What a death was from in a forager or village-farming society (Disease, "what a death among foragers or village farmers was from", `age`): Gurven & Kaplan 2007 (Popul Dev Rev 33:321, Table 5, pooled 3,328 deaths in 13 groups; read from the page image, counts sum to the printed totals): illness / degenerative / accident / violence under 15: 65.3, 9.5, 8.1, 17.1 %; 15–59: 60.5, 9.2, 12.9, 17.4 %; 60+: 54.6, 28.2, 10.3, 6.9 %. Illness splits into respiratory 23.7, gastrointestinal 13.8, fever 7.3, other 16.6, violence into homicide 6.3, warfare 5.2 (all-ages shares from the groups with good data, rescaled within their class [ASSUMED]). Check (20,000 draws, age 30): respiratory 23.3 %, gastrointestinal 13.6, other illness 16.3, fever 7.2, degenerative 9.1, accident 12.8, homicide 9.7, warfare 7.9.

### 6.16 Weather

Weather gives the day's rain and temperatures at one of 28 US stations from the story date, by WGEN (Richardson & Wright 1984, "WGEN: a model for generating daily weather variables", USDA ARS-8). Rain: a wet or dry day by a first-order Markov chain (monthly P(W/W), P(W/D)), the amount from a gamma distribution (monthly alpha, beta; Table A1, 20 years of daily data 1951–70). Max and min temperature and solar radiation: residuals χ_i = A χ_{i−1} + B ε_i with A and B of eqs. 10–11, value = m (χ c + 1), the mean m and coefficient of variation c following u + C cos(0.0172 (day − T)) on wet or dry days (Tables 1–5). Each station's year is generated from 1 January with a seed from the chat and the year, so the same date always gives the same weather. As in the program listing (Appendix C), a residual normal beyond ±2.5 is redrawn and a coefficient of variation below 0.06 is raised to 0.06. Check (30 years, Atlanta): January high 11.8 °C (SD 5.3), July high 31.6 °C (SD 3.2). Temperature through the day: lowest at 06:00, highest at 15:00, cosine between [ASSUMED]. Table A1 is an image scan: it was read by OCR twice (300 and 400 dpi) and the six values that differed between the passes or fell out of range were read off the scan (Albuquerque Feb P(W/W) 0.392 and P(W/D) 0.090, Atlanta Feb P(W/W) 0.490, Caribou Mar P(W/D) 0.315, Dodge City Dec P(W/D) 0.103, Boston Jan P(W/D) 0.333); Table 1 Grand Junction dry-day u read off the scan as 66.6 °F. Ely, Lander and Sault Ste. Marie have temperature tables but no readable rain row and are left out. Check (200 simulated years): Boston 1 125 mm of rain a year, January high 2.6 °C and low −5.0 °C, July high 27.6 °C; Phoenix 178 mm, 18.2 / 3.0 / 40.2 °C; Miami 1 502 mm; Bismarck 396 mm, January high −5.4 °C. These are close to the stations' published climate normals as recalled [not checked against a source here]. BodyEnvironment with `weather_station` puts characters outdoors at a station: their air (and radiant) temperature is set from the weather now, and PassTime then runs in hour steps, each taking the temperature at its start; any later BodyEnvironment call without the station (indoors, elsewhere) ends this. Story time is the tracker's [TIME] plus the minutes PassTime has run since [TIME] last changed, so several PassTime calls in one reply move the clock on. Check: a January morning in Bismarck from 06:00, hourly air temperature −16.8, −16.6, −16.2, −15.5, −14.7, −13.9, −13.1, −12.4, −12.0 °C.

Anywhere else: the Weather tool and BodyEnvironment take a town or city by name ("Lyon", "Sydney, Australia"), or latitude and longitude for a fictional or remote place (the nearest city is used, its distance reported). Cities: 1736 with all twelve months of mean daily maximum and minimum temperature, mean precipitation and mean number of precipitation days in the WMO World Weather Information Service (worldweather.wmo.int, supplied by each country's meteorological service; periods mostly 1961–1990, 1971–2000 or 1981–2010, many unstated; downloaded 11 October 2026; 22 dropped for impossible values). The 28 WGEN stations keep their full parameters when named. Monthly normals become WGEN parameters by SIMMETEO (Geng, Penning de Vries & Supit 1986, Agric For Meteorol 36:363; Geng et al. 1988), as coded in DSSAT's WGEN.for, subroutine SIMMIN (DSSAT Foundation, BSD-3):

| Parameter | Rule |
|---|---|
| wet-day share PW | precipitation days ÷ days in the month |
| P(W/D), P(W/W) | 0.75 PW; 1 − P(W/D) (1 − PW) ÷ PW |
| gamma β, α | rain per wet day R: β = max(−2.16 + 1.83 R, 0.01), α = R ÷ β within 0.01–0.998; when α would exceed 0.998, β = R ÷ 0.998 [ASSUMED: keeps the mean; SIMMETEO's cap alone gave Cairo 1 mm of January rain for a normal of 5] |
| max temperature, dry and wet days | mean ± split, split = \|9.67 − 27.4 × mean P(W/D)\| × 5/9 °C, weighted by the month's PW [DSSAT weights by the annual mean PW, which shifted monthly means by up to 1 °C] |
| SDs | max temperature max(5.8 − 0.09 T, 0.5); min temperature max(5.2 − 0.13 T, 0.5) °C |
| daily values | the WGEN residual chain (A, B, redraw beyond ±2.5); value = mean + SD × residual |
| through the year | temperature parameters linear between mid-months, mid-month values corrected until each month's mean equals its normal (DSSAT's CFACT); rain parameters the month's own |

Precipitation falls as snow when the day's mean air temperature is at or below 1.0 °C (Northern Hemisphere mean 50 % rain–snow threshold, Jennings et al. 2018, Nat Commun 9:1148; −0.4 to 2.4 °C by region) [day mean as air temperature: ASSUMED]. A place sets the latitude Look uses for daylight.

Check (30 simulated years; normal in brackets): London January high 8.3 (8.1), low 2.9 (3.1), rain 40 (42) mm on 11.3 (11.4) days; Tokyo October rain 228 (235); São Paulo January high 27.2 (27.3), rain 222 (239) on 18.8 (18) days; Lagos July rain 276 (257); Cairo July high 34.8 (34.8), dry. Moscow January high comes out −5.2 against −6.3: WGEN swaps a day's max and min when they cross, which raises the max where both spreads are large.

Real days, 1940 to about 6 days ago: Weather and BodyEnvironment (weather_place) first fetch that day's recorded weather at the place's coordinates from ERA5 (Hersbach et al. 2020, Q J R Meteorol Soc 146:1999, from 1950; back to 1940 by Soci et al. 2024, Q J R Meteorol Soc 150:4014; 0.25° grid, hourly) through the Open-Meteo historical API (free, no key, CC BY 4.0), read as Unix times (timeformat=unixtime) for the day before, the day and the day after, and set on the place's own clock on that date (the API applies one fixed offset, the one at request time, to every date; its documentation): air temperature, wind and humidity at each clock hour h:00, cloud cover at h:30 and global irradiance as the mean of the clock hour (ERA5 gives the mean of the hour ending at the time stamp); the light reads the hour's cloud and irradiance (§6.2). Day high and low, precipitation and snowfall are the day's totals; the temperature now is the hour's value; a wet day has ≥ 0.1 mm (WGEN's threshold); radiation MJ/m² × 23.9 = langleys. Snow is reported from ERA5 snowfall, not the 1.0 °C rule. Each day is fetched once per session and kept in memory; PassTime uses it for characters outdoors. A future day, a day before 1940, a WGEN station without coordinates or a failed call falls back to the generator above. Check: London 6 June 1944 high 13.5, low 9.3 °C, 0.8 mm, wind 5 m/s; Moscow 5 December 1941 high −20.6, low −26.1 °C; São Paulo 15 January 2000 high 30.6, low 21.1 °C, 5.5 mm.

Real days 1851–1939: Weather and BodyEnvironment fetch the day's 2 m maximum and minimum temperature and precipitation at the nearest grid point (about 1°) of the NOAA-CIRES-DOE Twentieth Century Reanalysis version 3 (Slivinski et al. 2019, Q J R Meteorol Soc 145:2876), one call per variable for the whole year, through NOAA PSL's daily time-series service (getdaily.pl; no daily values before 1851 there). Only surface pressure is assimilated, so temperature and rain are reconstructions; the reply says so. Rain = rate × 86,400 mm/day; snow when the day's mean ≤ 1.0 °C at 10 mm of snow per mm of water [ASSUMED]; air through the day on the generator's curve; no wind or humidity. The service sends no CORS header: in the browser it is read through SillyTavern's CORS proxy (`/proxy/` followed by the whole address URI-encoded, since the proxy takes the target from the path and drops the query string; `enableCorsProxy: true` in config.yaml); without it the generator with the past-climate shift answers and the reply says the reanalysis could not be reached. Check: London 1 January 1880 high 12.4, low 8.5 °C, 3.4 mm; 15 July 1880 21.7 / 12.8 °C, 5.9 mm; São Paulo 15 January 1900 20.3 / 19.6 °C, 0.7 mm.

Past climate: generated weather (no ERA5 day) is shifted by the country's recorded land-temperature anomaly for that month and year against the same calendar month's 1991–2020 mean, the base period of the WMO normals (Berkeley Earth regional TAVG files, Rohde & Hausfather 2020, Earth Syst Sci Data 12:3469; January 2021 run; 143 of the 144 normals countries, Maldives missing; 1750–2020 as coverage allows; months with 95 % uncertainty over 2 °C left out [ASSUMED]); high and low both move by the anomaly, rain unchanged. US WGEN stations are not shifted (their base period is not stated). Check: United Kingdom January 1963 −5.3 °C (the "Big Freeze"), December 2010 −4.5, August 1816 −2.8; London January 1963 mean generated high 2.7 °C against about 8 °C normal.

### 6.17 Choices

Choose draws a character's pick among options the narrator lists, each with the share of people in that character's place who would take it (the same labelled bands as BodyCheck), weighted random over all options; options with the same wording are merged, since a duplicated option takes extra share from the others in a logit choice (McFadden's independence of irrelevant alternatives) and keeps a floor of 1 % so no listed option is impossible (Mark 2009, Behavioral Mathematics for Game AI, ch. 16, "weighted random from all choices"), the probabilistic-choice view of McFadden's conditional logit (1974). Check: 80 % against 20 % gave 324 and 76 of 400 draws.

Each option can carry a `kind` (attack or fight; run, hide or keep away; take a risk for a gain; play safe or wait; help someone; talk, bargain or give way; something else), and the chooser's state moves its share (`choice.ts`). An effect measured as a standardized difference d is a log-odds shift of 1.81 d (Chinn 2000, Stat Med 19:3127). Traits: the share of the person's hours spent at the trait's pole against an average person's (Fleeson states; traits by sex and age, §6.9): attack by low agreeableness, flight by low emotional stability, help by high agreeableness, talk by high extraversion, playing safe by high conscientiousness [ASSUMED mapping]. Fear: d 0.30 toward the safe option (Wake, Wormwood & Satpute 2020, Cognition & Emotion, 52 effect sizes). Anger: risk taking d 0.54 in men, −0.21 in women, 0.17 when sex is unknown (Ferrer, Maclay, Litvak & Lerner 2017, Risk Analysis). Alcohol: aggression d 0.61 against a placebo (Bushman & Cooper 1990, Psychol Bull 107:341), in full at 0.08 % and in proportion below [ASSUMED dose line]. Fighting and running drop out when the body cannot stand under heavy exertion (the BodyCheck stance check). The answer names what weighed. An angry man at 0.08 % with no trait lean, given fight or talk at 50 % each, fights about 89 % of the time.

### 6.18 Battles

Not used: Dupuy's QJM factors for posture (hasty defence 1.3 to fortified 1.6), morale (excellent 1.0 to panic 0.2) and degrees of surprise (Numbers, Predictions and War, 1979, App. A, Tables 5, 7, 9) are his judgement fitted to history, and the CDB90 force-ratio shares below already average over defence postures; multiplying them in would count posture twice. They wait for an engagement set coded by posture.

Battle decides whether an attack on a defended position succeeds, from the force ratio (attackers ÷ defenders) and the year, using the shares of attacker wins in the Dupuy Institute's historical engagement databases (C. A. Lawrence, War by Numbers, 2017, tables as posted at dupuyinstitute.org under "Force Ratios"). Adjacent ratio bands with fewer than 30 cases are merged, cases weighted [stated process]; full data sets, not the culled ones.

| Years | Force ratio | Attacker wins | Cases |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1600–1900 (Battles Data Base, 243 battles) | under 1 | 54 % | 94 |
| | 1 to under 2 | 58 % | 109 |
| | 2 or more | 60 % | 40 |
| 1901–1991 (Division-level Engagement Data Base, 672 engagements, 1904–1991; 1901–1903 take this table [ASSUMED]) | under 1 | 39 % | 88 |
| | 1–1.49 | 55 % | 150 |
| | 1.5–1.99 | 59 % | 123 |
| | 2–2.49 | 71 % | 56 |
| | 2.5–2.99 | 83 % | 53 |
| | 3–3.49 | 69 % | 48 |
| | 3.5–3.99 | 77 % | 30 |
| | 4–5.99 | 65 % | 66 |
| | 6 or more | 95 % | 39 |

The dips at 3–3.5 and 4–6 are in the data and are kept. Before 1900 the ratio changes the result by 6 points across its range, so the result says that leadership, ground and morale decided the rest. Outside 1600–1991 the tool hands the decision to BodyCheck. BodyCheck tasks naming a battle, an assault or storming a position are routed to Battle. CDB90 (below) shows the same flat pattern before 1900: attacker wins 60, 62, 62, 35 and 61 % at ratios under 1, 1–1.5, 1.5–2, 2–3 and 3 or more (236 battles).

Surprise: when the defenders were unaware of the attack until it struck, the share is shifted in log-odds by the difference between attacks with and without attacker surprise in CDB90: 1600–1900 74 % (61 battles) against 56 % (162), shift +0.80; 1901–1973 82 % (105) against 64 % (271), shift +0.94 [ASSUMED: independent of the force ratio]. CDB90's surprise codes were assigned by historians after the battle, so the effect may be overstated. Example: 0.8 to 1 in 1757, 54 % → 72 %. Defender surprise (14 battles) and the defender's posture (fortified defences were attacked mostly by stronger forces, so posture and ratio are confounded) are not used.

Casualties and duration: copied from one real battle in CDB90 (US Army Concepts Analysis Agency, Database of Battles 1990; open copy github.com/jrnold/CDB90, whose cleaned files set every negative code to missing, so outcomes were re-read from the original WKS files: 398 attacker wins, 217 losses, 43 draws, 2 unknown) of the same era (1600–1900, 1901–1973) and outcome, chosen at random among the 10 nearest in force ratio and total size (distance |Δ ln ratio| + |Δ ln size|) [ASSUMED: k = 10, equal weights]. Battles with reinforcements are left out, as Helmbold 1995 (CAA-RP-95-1) did, leaving 427. Size enters because the loser's casualty share falls from 27 % in battles under 10 000 men to 12 % at 10 000–50 000. The battle's casualty shares (killed, wounded, captured, missing) are applied to the story's forces and its active fighting hours are reported. Medians, 1600–1900: attacker won: attackers 9 %, defenders 27 %, 8 hours; attacker lost: attackers 22 %, defenders 9 %. 1901–1973: won 3.5 % / 14 %, 48 hours; lost 4.6 % / 4.6 %. After 1973 the 1901–1973 battles are used [ASSUMED]. Draws (3 % before 1900, 10 % after) are folded into failures, as in the Dupuy tables.

### 6.19 Substances checked against their atoms

A substance record is a name, a formula and measured values (`rules/substances.json`). Code derives what the atoms fix and refuses records that contradict it (decided 2026-10-18, from the user: constrain by composition, fill gaps from the family, measured values win over family guesses, a broken conservation law means the record is wrong).

| Quantity | Rule | Source |
|---|---|---|
| molar mass | sum of atomic weights (H 1.008, C 12.011, N 14.007, O 15.999) | IUPAC conventional atomic weights |
| oxygen to burn it | mol O2 = C + H ÷ 4 − O ÷ 2 (to CO2, liquid water, N2) | element balance |
| heat of burning | ≈ 442 kJ per mol O2: median over the 68 records with 2 or more carbons; leave-one-out median error 1.8 %, 90 % within 7.1 % (propyne left out); a family with 3 or more measured members (fatty acids 437, alcohols) uses its own median [ASSUMED for any unmeasured substance] | Thornton 1917 rule, fitted on The Engineering ToolBox (2017) "Combustion Heat" table and Chemistry LibreTexts "Foods and Fuels" (glucose 15.6 kJ/g, valine 2922 kJ/mol) |
| verdict | measured vs atoms: within 10 % ok, 10–20 % suspect, beyond 20 % contradiction (refused, estimate used) [ASSUMED thresholds]; one-carbon molecules and H2 not judged (11–29 % off: their bonds differ from chains) | |
| food energy | carbohydrate 4, protein 4, fat 9, alcohol 7 kcal/g; parts heavier than the food refused; more than 9 kcal per g of food refused (pure fat is the ceiling); a stated kcal more than 20 % off the composition replaced by it [ASSUMED tolerance] | Atwater general factors, FAO 2003, Food and Nutrition Paper 77, ch. 3 |

Check (`node tools/check-substances.mjs`): 62 ok, 5 suspect, 1 contradiction, 11 not judged. The contradiction is propyne, printed 185 kJ/mol where its atoms allow 1768 (a misprint in the source table). Suspect: propanal printed 1522, atoms allow 1768 (−13.9 %, likely another misprint); acetylene, ketene (strained or triple bonds hold more energy, +16–18 %), dimethyl ether and methyl formate (+10 %). The table also prints 1-butanol as C4H10 (oxygen missing); its heat sits −7 % from what C4H10 allows, inside the ok band: a formula error that keeps the heat near the chain rule is not caught. Estimates: sucrose 15.5 kJ/g, stearic acid 39.9 kJ/g, leucine 27.8 kJ/g. Glucose measured 15.6 kJ/g, atoms allow 14.8 (−5 %).

Fix found by the same rule: alcohol drunk now counts 7 kcal/g toward food energy (before, it raised blood alcohol only). BodyIntake takes `food_g`, `carb_g`, `protein_g`, `fat_g`. Checks (harness): 50 g carbohydrate, 10 protein, 5 fat stated as 900 kcal → 285 kcal used; 2000 kcal in 100 g → 900 used; 60 g of parts in 50 g → refused.

Structure (added 2026-10-18, second pass). Records carry a SMILES string (`smiles`); `structure.ts` reads it (organic atoms, aromatic rings, brackets, branches, ring closures), counts every atom including implicit hydrogens, finds ring atoms, and sorts the atoms into the 40 Joback groups (Joback & Reid 1987, Chem Eng Commun 57:233; coefficients from the thermo library, MIT licence, `rules/joback.json`; nitro group left out). From the groups: boiling point (stated mean error 12.9 K), melting point (22.6 K), gas heat of formation (9.2 kJ/mol), heats of vaporisation (3.9 %) and fusion (38.7 %), heat capacity. Heat of burning by Hess's law: products CO2 −393.51, liquid water −285.83, SO2 −296.81 kJ/mol (CODATA, Cox, Wagman & Medvedev 1989); liquid = gas − vaporisation, crystal = gas − vaporisation − fusion [ASSUMED: values at the boiling and melting points stand for 298 K].

| Check | Rule |
|---|---|
| formula | the record's formula must equal the atoms of its structure, else contradiction |
| heat (structure) | Joback estimate: within 5 % ok, 5–10 % suspect, beyond 10 % contradiction [ASSUMED thresholds]; outside its domain a miss is at most suspect: one-carbon molecules, and crystals with N–H groups (amides, amino acids, urea: their hydrogen-bonded lattices hold energy Joback's vaporisation + fusion misses; 7–21 % low on this table) [ASSUMED domain] |
| heat (formula only) | the oxygen rule above |
| hazards | an O–O single bond is reported (peroxide: decomposes to oxygen and heat, an oxidiser); groups are not applied across it (outside the fitted compounds) |
| burns at all | oxygen demand ≤ 0 (H2O2: −0.5 mol O2) → does not burn |

Out-of-sample error on this table (Joback was fitted on other compounds): median 0.4 %, 90 % within 2.4 %, against 1.6 % and 6.1 % for the oxygen rule. Reader checked against RDKit 2026.09.1 + thermo 0.6.1: same formula and groups for all 79 records, and for 41 of 43 other molecules (halides, thiols, thiophene, pyrrole, indole, amines, esters, nitriles, sugars, amino acids); the 2 differences are deliberate: benzaldehyde gets the aldehyde group (thermo's pattern requires a non-aromatic neighbour carbon), hydrogen peroxide gets none (O–O). Anhydrides are refused, as in thermo.

Check (`node tools/check-substances.mjs`): 64 ok, 6 suspect, 3 contradictions, 6 not judged. Contradictions: 1-butanol (printed C4H10, its structure is C4H10O; the heat matches C4H10O within 0.3 %), propanal (printed 1522 kJ/mol, its groups give 1818: −16.3 %), propyne (185 vs 1939). Suspect: urea −20.9 %, formic acid −11.2 % (outside the domain), acetamide −8.4 %, alanine −6.6 %, cyclobutene +5.6 % (ring strain), methyl formate (formate esters fit no Joback group; oxygen rule +10.1 %). Estimates: sucrose 16.7 kJ/g, stearic acid 39.9, leucine 27.8; hydrogen peroxide: does not burn, O–O reported. Compounds (added 2026-10-18, third pass; `chem.ts`, `rules/chem.json`): 3796 single compounds with measured boiling and melting points, flash and autoignition points, flammability limits and heats of formation, as compiled by the chemicals library 1.5.2 (Caleb Bell, MIT licence) from CAS Common Chemistry, CRC Handbook, NIST WebBook, Yaws, Active Thermochemical Tables 1.112, NFPA 497 (2008), IEC 60079-20-1 (2010) and others (source kept per value). Structure reader at scale: all 3381 organic structures (C, H, N, O, S, halogens) read with no error and the same formula as RDKit; groups identical to thermo for 3138 of 3217, the rest deliberate (60 aromatic aldehydes given the aldehyde group, 13 peroxides refused, 6 coumarins). Boiling point estimate: Joback corrected by Stein & Brown 1994 (J Chem Inf Comput Sci 34:581; formula as recalled, confirmed by the fit here): mean error on 1854 measured compounds 21.8 K (Joback alone 44.6), median 12.2 K, 90 % within 45 K; above 12 groups 32.3 K (Joback 130.9). Melting point (Joback): mean error 67.7 K on 2620 compounds (melting depends on crystal packing, which groups do not see) — measured values are used wherever they exist.

| Physical check | Rule | Found in the 3796 |
|---|---|---|
| limits | 0 < lower < upper < 1 | 5 contradictions (a negative lower limit for 1-octanol, a 0 for acetophenone, upper limits above 1) |
| flash vs boiling | a pure liquid's flash point lies at or below its boiling point (+10 K tolerance [ASSUMED]) | 6 contradictions (ethane flash −29 °C above its boiling −89 °C; formaldehyde; both likely values for solutions or mixtures) |
| autoignition vs flash | ignition without a flame needs more heat than with one | 2 contradictions |
| melting vs boiling | melting above boiling = sublimes at 1 atm (CO2, hexachloroethane) or a wrong value (3-hexanol melts 185 °C, boils 134 °C as recorded) | 25 suspect |
| lower limit vs atoms | Jones 1938: LFL ≈ c ÷ (1 + 4.773 × mol O2), c = 0.49 here (median of 247; Jones 0.55); outside 0.25–1.0 suspect | 2 suspect |
| boiling vs groups | beyond 4 × the median error for its size (44 K, 77 K) | 167 suspect (mostly the estimate's own misses) |

A flash point derived from vapour pressure and the lower limit adds `flashShift` 2.5 K, the median of measured minus derived over the 236 compounds boiling above 25 °C (`vapour.ts`), registered as fitted with its bootstrap 95 % interval, 2.0–2.9 K (4000 resamples: median 2.47). Test R18.

Cross-check of two independent sources: heat of burning from the measured heat of formation (Hess) against the burning table of `substances.json`: 61 compounds, median difference 0.01 %; propyne 1938 vs the printed 185 confirms the misprint independently. Tool Substance (narrator): a compound by name or SMILES at the scene temperature → state, heat of burning, whether its vapour catches fire from a flame (temperature ≥ flash point), autoignition, limits, and any problems found. Example: acetone at −30 °C: liquid, 30.8 kJ/g, vapour not ignitable by a flame below −20 °C. Mixtures (kerosene, wood, food) are not single compounds and are refused. Fuels and mixtures (fourth pass). A solid fuel is its elements: higher heating value (dry, MJ/kg) = 0.3491 C + 1.1783 H + 0.1005 S − 0.1034 O − 0.0151 N − 0.0211 ash, wt % (Channiwala & Parikh 2002, Fuel 81:1051); wet: × (1 − water share); in practice (lower value) minus 2.443 MJ/kg for each kg of water present or formed from the hydrogen (8.937 × H). `rules/fuels.json`: wood, peat, lignite, bituminous coal, refuse-derived fuel (Speight, Synthetic Fuels Handbook, Table D.1, dry ash-free; measured HHV 20.2, 22.1, 23.3, 32.6, 22.6 MJ/kg). Checks: the formula gives these within 3 %; on 74 pure compounds median 2.7 % off, 90 % within 10.2 %; clean wood at 50 % water, 1.3 % ash: 8.0 / 9.8 MJ/kg vs 7.7 / 9.6 measured (Table D.3). Wood at 20 % water 14.5 MJ/kg, at 85 % 0.7. A mixture of compounds is given by mass ("ethanol 40%, water 60%"): heat = sum of the burning parts' heats × their shares (40 % ethanol: 11.9 kJ/g), elements summed, shares must add to 100. Vapour and liquid mixtures (fifth pass, `vapour.ts`). Vapour pressure for 1426 compounds from their tabulated equations (Perry's Handbook 8th ed. DIPPR 101; Poling, Prausnitz & O'Connell 5th ed. Antoine; Landolt-Börnstein Antoine; as compiled by the chemicals library); check: water at 20 °C 2.34 kPa. Flash point derived from first principles: the temperature where the vapour over the liquid reaches the lower flammability limit (vapour pressure = LFL × 101 325 Pa), + 2.5 K (the median by which closed-cup measurements sit above that temperature, fitted on the 236 compounds with both and a boiling point above 25 °C; median error 4.1 K, 90 % within 15.4 K); used when no flash point is measured, and as a check of measured ones (beyond 25 K suspect [ASSUMED]: 7 found, formaldehyde and cis-1,2-dichloroethene among them, the same two the boiling-point rule caught). Liquid mixtures: activity coefficients by original UNIFAC from each part's groups (Fredenslund, Jones & Prausnitz 1975; parameters of Hansen et al. 1991 and DDBST updates; group counts for 1117 compounds from the DDBST assignments; `rules/unifac.json`), same values as thermo's implementation to 4 digits (ethanol in water at 2 mol %: γ 6.248). Boiling point of the mixture where Σ x γ P = 1 atm, with the first vapour's composition; flash point by Liaw et al. 2002 (J Loss Prev Process Ind 15:429): Σ over burning parts x γ P(T) ÷ P(own flash point) = 1. Check against measured flash points of ethanol–water (The Engineering ToolBox, by weight): 30–96 % within 1.7 K (40 %: 25.7 vs 26 °C; 96 %: 13.5 vs 13), median error 1.5 K; dilute solutions come out low (20 %: 30.8 vs 36; 10 %: 38.8 vs 49; 5 %: 49.9 vs 62) [model limit; Liaw's form assumes the tabled limits hold in humid air]. A 40 % spirit: boils at 83 °C, first vapour 53 mol % ethanol, flash point 26 °C. Not done: kerosene and petrol as sets of compounds (needs a sourced surrogate composition); salts and metals; pressure and altitude (boiling points are at 1 atm).

**Below the melting point: liquid until a crystal starts (2026-10-28 (112); `freezing.ts`, `chem.ts`).** A temperature below a compound's melting point does not make a liquid solid: a crystal has to start in it, and until one does it stays a supercooled liquid. Substance used to answer "solid" from the temperature alone (T < Tm), so water at −1 °C was ice whatever it was. Now, for water, what decides is how much more readily H2O molecules leave the liquid than leave ice, the gap Δa_w between the water activity (1 for pure water) and ice's, a_w,ice(T) = p_ice ÷ p_liquid (vapour pressures over ice and supercooled water: Murphy & Koop 2005, QJRMS 131:1539; the criterion: Koop, Luo, Tsias & Peter 2000, Nature 406:611). Crystals start by themselves at log10 J = −906.7 + 8502 Δa_w − 26 924 Δa_w² + 29 180 Δa_w³ per cm³ per s (Koop et al. 2000); on particles in natural water, n(T) = e^(−0.7 T − 6.02) per mL start ice at T °C or warmer (fallen snow's meltwater, 88 samples: Brennan, David & Borduas-Dedekind 2020, ACP 20:163; fitted −6.3 to −15.0 °C, extrapolated outside and said so; [ASSUMED] the same spectrum for rain, lake, river and tap water), read at the pure-water temperature of the same Δa_w (Zobrist et al. 2008, J Phys Chem A 112:3965); ice the water touches (frost, snow) starts it at once. A volume V mL held t s freezes with P = 1 − exp(−(n V + J V t)), drawn once; when it freezes, c_p ΔT ÷ L of it turns to ice at once (c_p 4.22 J/(g K), L 333.6 J/g) and the rest warms to the melting point as slush. Inputs: `volume_ml`, `contents` (natural water; distilled or filtered; touching ice, frost or snow), `minutes` (`coldHoldMin` 1 min [ASSUMED] when not given; it counts only for crystals starting by themselves). Without a volume the answer is that ice stays ice and liquid water stays liquid until ice starts in it. Any other compound below its melting point: solid once crystallised, a melt stays liquid until a crystal starts, at a rate the table does not hold. Check (harness): the same water at −1 °C stays liquid as a cloud droplet (P 2 × 10⁻¹²) and freezes as a litre (P 99 %); a clean 10 µm droplet has a 50 % chance in 1 s at −37.1 °C (homogeneous freezing near −38 °C, Koop et al. 2000); 0.05 mL drops of snowmelt freeze half at −12.4 °C (Brennan et al.: −12.5 on average over their samples); a bucket at −10 °C freezes 13 % at once. Tried and not used: water activity of ethanol–water by the repository's UNIFAC (original parameters) puts the ice melting point of 39 wt % ethanol at −10.0 °C, against −28.7 °C measured (Lange 1967), so mixtures keep no freezing answer until an activity model fits at these temperatures. Controls RK1–RK8.

### 6.20 Actions and the clock

Decided 2026-10-18 with the user: the clock moves only by computed durations (duration = amount ÷ rate); one clock for everyone; positions are state, not words, so "John leaves his house" takes longer from the bedroom than from the doorway; a time digit is shown only when a process gives it. Tool Act (`actions.ts`): a character's steps in order.

| Step | Duration | Source |
|---|---|---|
| walk_to a spot | recorded distance ÷ own walking speed (§6.7a: age, sex, country, slope, load) | §6.7a |
| sit 34.7, stand from sitting 43.4, bend / stoop / kneel on one knee 29.0, arise 31.9, kneel on both knees 69.4, arise 76.7, turn the body 45–90° 18.6, sidestep 17.0, grasp 2.0, release 2.0, focus the eyes 7.3 TMU | × 0.036 s per TMU | MTM-1 data card, MTM Association 2019 (experienced person, normal effort) |
| reach | 6.1 TMU at 10 cm to 18.2 at 80 cm, linear between [interpolation ASSUMED], +0.24 TMU per cm beyond [ASSUMED] | MTM-1, case A |

Positions: `be_at` records a spot ("John's house: front door"); `walk_to` needs the distance the first time two spots are joined (`distance_m`, from the scene), keeps it in the chat state and reuses it, ignoring a later different figure (said in the result). Clock: the story clock gains each action's exact seconds and shows hh:mm:ss once seconds are non-zero; bodies (bleeding, food, heat) run in whole minutes, the remaining seconds carried to the next action. Precision: each step's duration is reported to the digit its spread allows, spread 10 % [ASSUMED] (1.6 s, 29 s, 18 min); the clock itself keeps the exact sum, which reproduces from the steps (the check of invertibility). The audit now flags a clock time or action duration in the narration that no PassTime or Act result gives. Cross-check: MTM's 15.0 TMU (0.54 s) per pace and the gait speed 1.43 m/s of a man in his 30s imply a 0.77 m step. Example (man of 35, Paris): stand 1.6 s, bedroom → living room 6 m 4.4 s, → front door 8 m 5.9 s, reach 50 cm 0.47 s, grasp 0.072 s, → street 40 m 29 s: 42 s, 10:18:00 → 10:18:42; from the doorway the same exit is 29 s. Several characters at once (borrowed from the intentions-then-resolution tick of Thing' Sandbox, github.com/zebrr/Thing-Sandbox): `actors` holds each character's steps; all are timed on one clock, the span is the slowest actor's, all actors count as active for the body, and a merged timeline says who finishes what first (café: Marie reaches the door and grasps the handle at 3.5 s, John from his table at 8.2 s). A failed step undoes the whole call. Stairs and crowds (SFPE emergency-movement model, Nelson & Mowrer, SFPE Handbook, k table as given in the Pathfinder technical reference, Thunderhead Engineering): speed along the line of travel ∝ k (1 − 0.266 D) [equation as recalled], unimpeded below 0.54 persons/m²; k level 1.40, stairs 7.5/10 in 1.00, 7/11 1.08, 6.5/12 1.16, 6.5/13 1.23 (linear to 1.40 at slope 0; extended beyond 0.75 down to 0.034). The walker's own speed is scaled by k ÷ 1.40 and by the crowd ratio (D 1: × 0.86, 2: × 0.55, 3.55 cap: × 0.07). Going up is also held to the climb their energy allows at full aerobic power (§6.7a cost at the stair's grade, at Wu & Wang's limit for relative load 1) [ASSUMED]: man of 35, 0.51 m/s along a 7/11 stair (a 16-step storey in 10 s); woman of 75, 0.18 m/s. Steps `stairs_up` / `stairs_down` (steps, riser_cm, tread_cm; default 17.8 / 27.9 cm [ASSUMED typical]), `crowd_per_m2` on walks and stairs. Not done: door opening as its own step, doorway flow limits in a crowd, two actors contending for one thing (both reach the door: who gets it is the timeline's order, not a rule), sound travel time between spots, the scene layout drawn from building data when no distance is given.

### 6.20a The map

Tool Layout (`map.ts`): spots get a position (x, y in metres on the scene's plan, floor number) and rooms an outline (corners, floor, ceiling height). From the plan: room floor area (shoelace) and volume, used by Room when not given; a spot's room (stated, or the outline that contains it), so people at a spot inside a room breathe its air; walking between placed spots with no recorded distance = straight line × 1.2 [ASSUMED route factor, registered 1.0–1.5; Boeing 2019 (arXiv 1708.00836) measures walking networks as more direct than driving ones, with no single figure], plus a storey of stairs (16 steps) per floor [ASSUMED]; Communicate and Look take the distance between two placed people, and people in different rooms with no open doorway between hear through a plaster wall [ASSUMED default] and cannot see each other. Law: a recorded walking distance is never shorter than the straight line between placed spots (storey height 2.8 m [ASSUMED]). Check (harness): kitchen 12 m², parlour 20 m², bedroom upstairs 16 m²; John at the stove, Mary in the armchair 7.3 m away: through the wall almost no words; with the doorway open every word, and she can see him; stove → bed upstairs 11 s (stairs 8.6 s + 2.7 m); a 3 m distance to the armchair refused (6.6 m straight line).

**Where people stand is the state's (2026-10-28 (111); `rules-tools.ts` placed, `body-engine.ts` reachOf).** When both people stand at spots on the map, the distance and any wall between them come from the map, not from the call: BodyStrike's distance (before: within reach by default, so a punch landed between people placed 6 m apart; a given distance was used as given), and a shut wall between stops the blow; BodyChoke needs the holder within reach (0.88 m at 70 kg, FAA 2006-08 functional reach, men's 50th percentile, scaled by body length ∝ mass^1/3) and says where both stand; BodyCheck's handgun `shot_m` toward a tracked `shot_at`, and its wall; Chase's `gap_m`; Communicate's and Look's distance (this section already said they take it; before, a given figure replaced the map's); Listen's new `source_who`, which sets the distance and, through a shut wall, a plaster wall [ASSUMED default, as for Communicate]. A given figure that differs is ignored and named in the result ("map distance 6 m kept; 0.5 m ignored"), as Act keeps a recorded walk and BodyFall the height stood at. A blow out of reach names both spots and the Act that closes the distance. Check (harness, `tests/engine.test.mjs`): Mara at the bar, Tom at the hearth 6 m away: a punch given 0.5 m stays out of reach (6 m); Ned on the stool 0.5 m away: in reach; the cook in the larder, a wall with no doorway between: no blow, no shot, a voice through plaster at 9.5 m; a shot at Ned given at 300 m (0.0 % to hit) is taken at the map's 0.5 m (99.6 % at 1 m, feats.ts) and hits; a chase given 20 m runs from the map's 6 m. Controls RJ1–RJ11.

### 6.21 History and memory

World history as diffs (`world-log.ts`; borrowed from the plan → diff → validate → apply cycle of Huang, Li & Fang 2026, "Orchestrated Reality", arXiv 2606.16014): every tool call that changes the state stores the paths it changed and their new values, with the tool, its arguments, its random draws and the message it belongs to; setting the time is stored the same way. The last 400 changes are kept (older ones folded into the base). A swipe drops the rewritten message's changes with its state. Swiping back to an earlier swipe (no generation runs) takes the turn's changes as the later swipe made them out of the record and adds one entry, "swipe shown", holding what the swipe shown changed in the turn, so the replay rebuilds the world shown; the turns before keep their entries (2026-10-26 (99), R31). `/rules-replay` replays the changes on the base and says whether they rebuild the current state exactly; a difference means something changed the world outside the tools (check, harness: 6 changes replay exactly; setting a body mass by hand is caught at people.John.mass).

Laws checked after every call (`invariants.ts`; the validate step of plan → diff → validate → apply): time only runs forward (except setting the time); the dead stay dead; mass above 0, blood lost between 0 and the whole volume, water and food deficits not negative, room gases between 0 and 100 % with oxygen at most the outside 20.95 %; items conserved except a Transfer from or to nobody and a Claim; no recorded walking distance longer than a route through other recorded spots. A call that breaks one is undone and refused with the reason, so the history holds only lawful states (check, harness: a 50 m A → C refused when A → B → C is 10 m; all earlier scenarios pass). Walking between spots with no direct distance takes the shortest route through recorded ones (Dijkstra) and says the way (C → A via B, 10 m).

Constants with their origin (`constants.ts`, listed in `docs/constants.md`, generated by `node tools/constants-doc.mjs`): a number is declared once with value, unit, status (sourced, fitted, assumed, raw) and, when judged, a plausible range; reading it records that the call used it. After a call passes the laws, it is rerun on the same state and the same draws with each judged constant it used at both ends of its range (up to 6 constants; not while a Pulse track runs); when the outcome line changes (numbers aside), the result ends "Fragile: …" naming the value it rests on. Check (harness): a sleeper in a 22 m³ hut with a 1.5 kg/h wood fire faints at 64 min 48 s; at 15 g CO per kg (the low end of the measured range instead of the 50 midpoint) he sleeps through 3 hours: reported fragile on that value. Registered: 119 (docs/constants.md lists them; every judged one carries a range that holds its value, tests/engine.test.mjs N53). A value counts as used once it can change the result: `peek()` reads it without counting, `reaches(id, x)` asks whether x reaches a threshold and counts the threshold only when x lies within its range (outside it every value in the range answers alike), and a computation counts its values from the point they start to matter (the crowd below). A costly computation is kept by `memo()` with the values it read; a rerun that varies another value gets it back at once, one that varies a value it read computes afresh (N50). Added this way in 2026-10-23 (7): breath-hold spread, minutes to double the odds on a task, onset of dehydration effects, the size of a meal for alcohol, the swim-skills share, the spread of one try at a feat, the lift's correlation with grip, conformity to a majority of one, the crowd's jostle and courtesy time; the leg-fracture spread got its range. Added in 2026-10-26 (98), from an outside review: the shares of falls on the level that end in a serious injury under 45 and at 45–64 (slipUnder45, its range from McNamee et al.'s fracture ratio 3.35–4.75; slipMiddleAge) and the peak of a warm-blooded creature's heat production (endoPeak, 4.9 × resting, range 4–8 from Minnaar et al. 2014, read only where the cap can bind); a slip whose draw falls inside the range now says it is fragile (R30). Older modules keep further constants in comments until moved; `docs/constants.md` lists each such tag under "Assumptions not yet registered".

Fragility census (`node tools/fragility-census.mjs`): six scenes run through the real tools, counting which registered judgement each outcome rests on. 2026-10-18: charcoal brazier in an attic, a parlour fire, 20 people in a closed 40 m³ room for 4 h, an uphill walk with a 25 kg load: robust (no judgement in range changes the outcome); a wood fire in a small hut: rests on the wood CO yield; a brazier in the next room with the door open: rests on the doorway coefficient, the doorway's minimum temperature difference and charcoal's carbon share. Those four are where a better source changes stories.

Event moments inside a minute (`advanceAll`): the body runs in whole minutes, but an event is placed where its quantity crossed the threshold, taking the quantity as changing evenly over that minute [ASSUMED]: blood lost (classes at 15, 30, 40 %), carboxyhaemoglobin (30 % unconscious, 40 % dead), water deficit (2, 5, 8 % of body mass), work done (the day's limit), core temperature (40 °C rising; 35, 32, 28, 24 falling); other events (infection, sepsis, altitude illness) at the minute's end. Not a full event queue: processes still step by the minute; a queue needs each process to state its next change, which most rules cannot yet. The clock stops at that second ("49 min 2 s"); the bodies hold their end-of-minute state [ASSUMED: under a minute's difference].

Memory (borrowed from Park et al. 2023, "Generative Agents: Interactive Simulacra of Human Behavior", UIST, §4.1): when a character knows more facts than [KNOWS] shows (12), the ones shown are those with the highest recency + importance + relevance, each scaled 0–1 over that character's facts, equal weights. Recency × 0.995 per story hour since learned (Park counts since last retrieved [ASSUMED: learned]); importance 1–10 given by RecordFact `importance` on Park's scale (1 mundane, 10 extremely poignant), 5 when not given [ASSUMED]; relevance = share of the fact's content words in the last two messages [ASSUMED: word overlap in place of embedding similarity, which needs a model call]. Shown oldest first. Check (harness, 20 facts over 7.5 story days): a scene about bread keeps the 7-day-old bakery fire (relevant) and the brother's death (importance 10), and drops the 8 oldest meals (importance 1).

### 6.22 Room air

Tool Room (`rooms.ts`): an enclosed space, keyed by the same spot name as Act, as a well-mixed box. Each minute (PassTime and Act run it before the bodies) every gas follows dC/dt = G ÷ V − λ (C − C outside), solved exactly over the minute, λ = air changes per hour ÷ 60; everyone whose spot is the room breathes its carbon monoxide (§6.8 uptake) and its oxygen (below 20.9 % as the equivalent altitude, §6.6); leaving the room returns them to clean air.

| Quantity | Rule | Source |
|---|---|---|
| air changes | lognormal, median 0.45 per hour, 10th percentile 0.18 → σ 0.715; drawn once per room unless the story sets it [ASSUMED: any era and country] | US EPA Exposure Factors Handbook 2011, ch. 19, Table 19-1 (2,844 tracer measurements; Koontz & Rector 1995; lognormal fit: Murray & Burmaster 1995, Risk Anal 15:459) |
| fire | per kg of fuel, from its elements: carbon → CO2 except what leaves as CO; O2 taken = C + H ÷ 4 − O ÷ 2 (moles), less half a mole per CO; wood C 50, H 6, O 44 %; charcoal pure carbon [ASSUMED]; a compound burnt in a lamp from its formula | Speight, Synthetic Fuels Handbook, Table D.1 |
| carbon monoxide | charcoal 276 g per kg burnt; other solid fuels 15–85 g → 50 used [ASSUMED]; liquids and gases none unless given [ASSUMED] | IPCC Emission Factor Database meeting report, India, Annex 7 (household stove tests) |
| breathing | O2 used = METs × 3.5 mL/kg/min; CO2 out 0.85 × that [ASSUMED respiratory exchange ratio]; 24.04 L per mole at 20 °C | |
| outside CO2 | 420 ppm unless given (about 280 before 1800) [ASSUMED; no year table yet] | |

Check (harness): a 30 m³ attic room, 0.3 changes per hour, 0.5 kg/h of charcoal, a man asleep: CO 2,911 ppm after 50 min, carboxyhaemoglobin 31 %, unconscious; dead at 59 min (40 %); O2 fall equivalent to 951 m altitude. By hand: 0.5 × 276 = 138 g CO/h = 118 L/h into 30 m³ ≈ 3,300 ppm in 50 min without leaks; the leak takes about 10 %. Replay of the world history exact. Fire heat and smoke (second pass). Heat release = fuel per second × its lower heating value (§6.19: wood from its elements at its water content, default 20 %; compounds from their heat of formation less the water's latent heat) [ASSUMED complete]. With an opening (door or window: area, height): air drawn in 0.5 A_o √H_o kg/s (Kawagoe 1958) sets the room's air changes when larger than its leak, and caps the fire at what that air's oxygen burns, 1,500 kW per unit of A_o √H_o (0.23 kg O2 per kg air × 13.1 MJ per kg O2, Huggett 1980); hot-layer temperature by McCaffrey, Quintiere & Harkleroad 1981 (ΔT = 6.85 [Q² ÷ (A_o √H_o A_T h_k)]^(1/3), h_k = √(kρc ÷ t) until the heat soaks through the lining, then k ÷ δ; US NRC NUREG-1805 ch. 2), layer held at the opening's top [ASSUMED]. Closed: one-zone heat balance (exact over each minute) and the smoke layer's lower edge by Yamana & Tanaka 1985 (as in NUREG-1805), the smoke staying after the flames go out. Flames go out below 15 % oxygen [ASSUMED]. Flashover reported at 600 °C. Occupants: a head in the layer (1.6 m standing, 0.3 m lying [ASSUMED]) breathes its temperature; radiant temperature from the layer, half the view when below it (T_r⁴ = 0.5 T_layer⁴ + 0.5 T0⁴ [ASSUMED]); JOS-3 and the burn rules then act. Linings: concrete k 0.0016 kW/m·K, ρ 2400, c 0.75 (NUREG-1805 worksheet); gypsum board, brick, pine [RAW: recalled from NUREG-1805 Table 2-3]; thicknesses [ASSUMED]. Checks (harness): parlour 16 m², 2.4 m, gypsum, door 1.6 m² open, 120 kg/h of wood = 482 kW: layer 251 °C at 2 min, 376 °C at 10 min; the man standing below the 2 m layer breathes 20 °C air, his skin reaches 40 °C and burns appear by 10 min (radiant); by hand, 500 kW at 10 min gives 385 °C (thin gypsum soaked through at 6.6 min). Closed brick cellar 12 m², 3 kg/h of wood (12 kW): smoke to 0.08 m above the floor in 10 min, oxygen 15 % and flames out at about 25 min, CO 2,500 ppm left. Between rooms (third pass): Room `doorways` joins rooms through open doors; each minute the two rooms swap Q × 60 s of air (gas conserved), Q = C (W ÷ 3) √(g ΔT ÷ T̄) H^(3/2) each way (Brown & Solvason 1962, Int J Heat Mass Transfer 5:859, eq. 6, checked against the paper supplied by the user), C = 0.8 read from their Fig. 5 (measured C between 0.6 and 1.0, rising with the Grashof number; 0.8 at 10⁸ with thin walls; a door at about 10⁹ lies beyond the tested range) [read from the figure, extrapolated]; the paper finds heat and mass carried alike; ΔT at least 1 K [ASSUMED: drafts and body heat]; a 1.6 m² door between rooms at the same temperature swaps 398 m³/h. Check (harness): charcoal brazier 0.6 kg/h in a 35 m³ kitchen, door open to a 30 m³ bedroom where a woman sleeps: bedroom CO 921 ppm at 30 min, 2,005 at 60 (she is unconscious, carboxyhaemoglobin 30 %), dead by 2 h. Not done: layering of gases other than smoke (the CO box is the room average), heat and smoke-layer flow through doorways (only gases move), smoke's soot and irritants, carbon dioxide's own effects on the body, gas flowing between connected rooms, open windows as a rule (only as a stated rate), outside CO2 by year.


Leaving through one exit (Room `exit_width_m`, `exit_pace`; `crowd.ts`): the social force model of escape panic run in code (Helbing, Farkas & Vicsek 2000, Nature 407:487): mass 80 kg, radius 0.25–0.35 m, acceleration time 0.5 s, repulsion 2000 N × e^((r − d) ÷ 0.08 m), body force 1.2 × 10⁵ and sliding friction 2.4 × 10⁵ on contact; a person is crushed and stops, an obstacle, when the contact forces over their circumference pass 1600 N/m, averaged over 1 s [ASSUMED averaging]. Paces: relaxed 0.6, normal 1, nervous 1.5, panic 5 m/s. People: the room's crowd plus the tracked people inside, up to 300; floor from the outline or `floor_m2`, a square with the exit mid-wall [ASSUMED shape]; a small jostle (±40 N) and calm people giving way after 3 s locked at the door [ASSUMED] keep two people from jamming for ever. Death in the press by compressive asphyxia: 6,227 N on the chest killed in 15 s and 1,112 N in 4–6 minutes (Hopkins, Pountney, Hayes & Sheppard 1993, Crowd pressure monitoring, in Smith & Dickie, Engineering for Crowd Safety, as given by G. K. Still); through both points the time to death is 15 s × (6,227 ÷ F)^1.74 [FITTED], each second adding its share of a lethal dose, nothing below the 623 N of first discomfort against a bar (same source) [ASSUMED], no recovery within the evacuation [ASSUMED]; the chest force is half the summed contact forces on the person, squeezed from two sides [ASSUMED]. The model's discs (0.5–0.7 m) start apart only up to about 2 a square metre: a denser crowd is worked out on the floor it needs, spread back from the exit, and Room says so [ASSUMED]. Tracked people inside are among those worked out, each at a place in the crowd drawn at random, and their end is applied: dead in the press (dead, compressive asphyxia), crushed and down (a blunt injury to the upper body), out after so many seconds, or still inside. Check (100 people, 15 × 15 m, 1 m door): 0.8 m/s 0.67 a second (the paper's calibration 0.73), no one crushed; 1.5 m/s 1.30; 3 m/s 1.03, 10 % crushed; 5 m/s 0.65, 60 % crushed ("faster is slower"); no deaths. 200 people in the same room: 1.5 m/s 1 crushed, 2 m/s 25, 3 m/s about half, 5 m/s about 80 %; 0–1 dead (more when the crush is held longer: up to 27 at 7 m/s with a 5 s average). The paper's Figure 1c has injuries begin only near 5 m/s for those 200; here they begin by 2–3 m/s, in the first seconds as the crowd reaches the exit wall, and this held with no jostle, no speed cap, uncapped friction and a crush pressure averaged over 5 s, so the crushed counts run high. Replications of the model do not reproduce the original's numbers either: Lakoba, Kaup & Finkelstein (2005, Simulation 81:339) found the original contact stiffness gives about 6,000 N for two people squeezed 5 cm each, unbounded squeezing and a stiff system, and their run put the fastest exit near 3 m/s, not 1.5; they proposed k = 2.4 × 10⁴, B = 0.5 m and a 7 cm cap on squeezing. The engine keeps the original parameters (they carry the 0.73 calibration) and reports the onset as the model's.

Computation (2026-10-23 (7)): each pair of people is worked out once, equal and opposite (Newton's third law), and a pair more than 0.6 m apart beyond contact is left out (its repulsion, 2000 × e^(−0.6 ÷ 0.08) = 1.1 N, against a drive of 80 N or more); walls are worked out only for someone within reach of one; square roots replace `Math.hypot`. The crowd draws from a stream of its own (mulberry32) seeded by one draw of the call: before, its 2 draws per person per 4 ms step went into the turn's trace (2.9 million draws and 56 MB of chat metadata for 100 people leaving; now about 100 draws, 2.5 KB). The averaging time counts as used from the first pressure past 1600 N/m (an average of pressures all below it stays below it whatever the time); the four asphyxia values count from the first moment someone's dose reaches 1 at the shortest time to death over all four ranges; the courtesy time from the first person locked at the door for 2 s (its lowest end). A calm crowd is then not rerun over values that cannot change it, and the values it did not count give the identical run at their range ends (N49). The jostle (40 N, range 20–80) and courtesy time (3 s, 2–5) are registered. Check, 6 seeds each (mean ± SD; before → now): 100 people at 0.8 m/s 0.67 ± 0.02 → 0.64 ± 0.03 a second, none crushed; 1.5 m/s 1.26 ± 0.05 → 1.27 ± 0.06; 5 m/s 41.2 ± 1.3 → 42.0 ± 1.8 out, flow 0.69 ± 0.20 → 0.68 ± 0.17; 200 people at 3 m/s 100.7 ± 2.7 → 99.2 ± 6.8 out; 5 m/s 41.3 ± 2.3 → 42.0 ± 2.3 out. Time for those 30 runs 222 s → 53 s (4.2 ×); a 100-person Room exit call with its sensitivity reruns about 3.5 s. A step of 8 ms halves the time again but moved 200 people at 3 m/s to 105 ± 6 out: the 4 ms step stays (`DT_S`).

## 7. Tools

| Tool | Change |
|---|---|
| all | species and mass on first appearance: the tool arguments, else the State Tracker profile (`Name (N kg)` header and its `Species:` line), else human at the species' adult mass |
| all (argument) | `species`: DF creature id or name ("HUMAN", "wolf", "giant wolf"); used when the character first appears; default human |
| BodyStrike | `part` = a part name of the target (free text); `attack` = the attacker's natural attack (punch, kick, bite, scratch…) or the weapon's attack verb (slash, stab, bash…); `weapon` = DF weapon name; `material` = metal; `skill` = DF level name; `style`; `prone`; `attacker_species`. Removed: `means`, untrained/trained/elite |
| BodyWound | `part` = a part name; cut, puncture or bite: a wound through every layer [ASSUMED]: vessel class as §5, and organ (`FUNCTIONAL`) tissue stops working, for good without `HEALING_RATE`, as in §5 |
| BodyCheck | `uses` = grasp, stance, sight, hearing, smell, breathing |
| BodyChoke | `who`, `holder`, `seconds` held: §6.1 choke rows |
| BodyEnvironment | `who` list; air °C, radiant °C, humidity, weather wind km/h or indoor air speed, ensemble and/or garments with covers, face covered, METs; `clear`: §6.4 |
| unknown part / attack / species | the tool returns the valid names; nothing recorded |

### 7.1 Audit: what the narrating model still decides

Each tool input is either a scene fact (visible in the story: distance, temperature, who is present) or an outcome guess (the model estimates how likely or how bad something is). Outcome guesses are where a narrator bends a scene; the goal is none.

| Tool | Input | Kind | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| BodyCheck | `share` (fraction who succeed) | outcome guess | jumps across a gap, lifts from the floor and handgun shots come from the body and the scene (§6.10); otherwise open: the main remaining guess (OPEN-90); chosen from 7 labelled bands (95, 80, 65, 50, 35, 20, 5 %) [ASSUMED values]: words and bands are the verbal-confidence form models can be taught to calibrate (Lin, Hilton & Evans 2022, TMLR), while their unprompted verbal confidence clusters at 80–100 % and hedges toward the middle (Xiong et al. 2024, ICLR; Geng et al. 2024, NAACL survey); Kadavath et al. 2022 measured calibration from answer probabilities, which a narrator cannot report. The narrator first names a reference class and its base share, then its own estimate; chance = the equal-weight average of the two in log-odds, logit⁻¹((logit class share + logit estimate) / 2). Kahneman & Tversky's (1977) shrinkage ρ = 2p − 1 is dropped: it needs p, the narrator's own ordering accuracy, and models are overconfident about it; averaging an LLM estimate with a second forecast in log-odds improved accuracy over either alone (Schoenegger et al. 2024, Science Advances; Halawi et al. 2024, NeurIPS), and log-odds averaging keeps confident inputs from being pulled to the middle as a plain average does (Baron et al. 2014, Decision Analysis; Lichtendahl et al. 2013, Management Science) |
| BodyCheck | task text | routing | the task goes to the tool that decides it, read from the task's own verb by its present-day meaning (`verbs.ts`, TASK_ROUTES, from VerbNet 3.4's classes, 2026-10-23 (9)): hearing (hear, listen, overhear, eavesdrop; catching or making out what is said) → Listen or Communicate; seeing (35 verbs of the perception classes 30.1–30.3 and watch) and searching (search, comb, scour, rummage, ransack, scan; going, rifling or sifting through) → Look; swimming, drowning → BodyEnvironment; hanging on and carrying (clasp, clutch, grasp, grip, cling, carry, haul, lug, tote; hanging or dangling from; holding on) → HoldOn; racing on foot (chase, pursue, flee, outrun; running away or off; catching up) → Chase; deceiving (trick-59.2: bluff, con, deceive, dupe, fool, hoodwink, mislead, trick; lying to or about) → SocialResponse; courting (court, flirt, woo, seduce; chatting up, asking out) and winning over (lure-59.3: cajole, coax, entice, flatter, lure; persuade, bribe, threaten, beg) → SocialResponse; taking a defended position (attack, storm, besiege, take, capture… a fort, town, hill, bridge) → Battle; a choke on the neck → BodyChoke; shooting (shoot; firing, loosing or aiming at something or with a missile weapon) and blows at a creature (VerbNet's hitting, swatting, spanking, poking, attacking, killing and cutting classes, less the members that touch without striking or work material: tap, rap, bump, saw, scrape, dig) → BodyStrike; treating a wound (bandage, stanch, stitch, suture, splint; dressing, treating, binding or setting a wound or limb) → BodyTreat. The verb is read where the task puts one, past try and the other helpers; a verb read in another of its senses keeps the task (lie down, bolt the door, kick the door open, hang the lantern, shoot the rapids, fire the kiln), and so does a verb BodyCheck decides itself (climb, jump, lift, pick, ride: "climb the wall to see over it"). With no verb read, words naming another tool's question still route it (noise; afloat; a shot at; a tourniquet; attracted; battle). Not by roots: roots keep old senses (discern < cernere "sift"; observe < servare "watch, keep safe", not servire "serve", Etymonline), and words of one meaning now come from different roots. Before, ten word lists let 43 of 77 phrasings run as checks on the narrator's share (listening, overheard, swam, clinging, fled, lied about, fool, punch, stab, bandage, court, besiege, search…) and sent 6 to the wrong tool (lie down → SocialResponse, bolt the door and a shot of whisky → BodyStrike, date the letter → SocialResponse, assault the guard → Battle). Lists of members chosen from a class [ASSUMED selection]. Check: N55, N56 (101 phrasings); `override: true` passes a task that is BodyCheck's after all. The caller also names what the task is (`task_kind`, required, from a closed list: a skill or effort of the body, or one of the routes' questions, reshuffled before every generation as the share bands are): a named route goes to its tool, and a kind and a verb that disagree are refused with both readings ("you named it seeing (Look), and its verb 'punch' reads as BodyStrike"); N59. A handgun shot at a person stays BodyCheck's with `shot_m`; the route names it. What VerbNet's classes leave out, FrameNet 1.7's frames route (2026-10-23 (11); `data/framenet.ts`, 651 lexical units of 30 frames, built by tools/build-framenet.mjs): verbs VerbNet lacks (befriend, elude, smother, spear, bayonet, resuscitate, invade), the noun a light verb carries (take a look, steal a glance, have a peek, give the room a once-over, get a glimpse; throw a punch, take a swing, take aim, from a short list FrameNet lacks [ASSUMED]), a noun opening the task (a quick glance at the window); its climbing verbs (climb, clamber, scramble) stay BodyCheck's, and a light verb whose noun names no tool's act stays too (take a nap). Each frame route names the frame's elements on the tool's arguments (Cause_harm: Agent → attacker, Victim → who, Body_part → part, Instrument → weapon), and every element named is FrameNet's own (N65). Members chosen where a frame also holds other acts (Perception_active holds smell and taste; Cause_harm holds poison and burn) [ASSUMED selection]. The router and the player's line read the same frames ("I take a look around": Look, not a taking; "I clamber up the wall": BodyCheck; "I elude the guards": Chase). |
| BodyCheck | `uses`, `exertion`, `on_feet`, `timed`, `fine` | scene facts (what the task involves) | — |
| BodyWound | `type`, `depth` for accidents | outcome guess | partly replaced: burns by BodyContact and heat, falls by BodyFall, attacks by BodyStrike |
| BodyStrike | `noise_db` | estimate | `noise_sound` (Listen presets, level from distance) replaces it when the sound is listed |
| BodyStrike | `skill` | character fact | — |
| CastSpell | `complexity` | setting fact (spell list) | — |
| BodyIntake | amounts | scene facts, with examples | — |
| BodyEnvironment | `co_ppm` | estimate | ranges given in the description |
| Look | `size_m`, `distance_m`, `light`, `visibility_m`, `weapon_in_view`, `dressed`, `against`, `seconds` | scene facts | a sighting of a tracked person is kept with the viewer's fear and a weapon in view, for lineups (§6.9) |
| Listen, Communicate | `sound`/`spl_db`, `distance_m`, `noise`, `barrier`, `effort` | scene facts (presets) | — |
| SocialResponse | `situation`, `is_lie`, `others`, `emergency`, `order`, `many_options`, `ally`, `target_who`, `culprit_present`, `other_group`, `sequential`, `earlier`, `same_requester`, `later_day`, `knows_speaker`, `expert_listener`, `high_stakes` | story and scene facts | a group's majority from the map; a lineup from the witness's Look and the hours since (§6.9) |
| BodyContact | `material`, `temp_c`, `seconds` | scene facts | — |
| BodyFall | `height_m`, `soft`, `head_first` | scene facts | — |
| HoldOn | `hanging`, `load_kg`, `hands` | scene facts | — |
| Chase | `gap_m` | scene fact | — |
| NewCharacter | `sex`, `occupation`, `min_age` | story facts (only when fixed) | sex, age and occupation from the 1881 census in England and Wales; elsewhere age and sex from the country's stable population (§6.11) |
| Look | `light` | scene fact (artificial light, overcast) | outdoors computed from date, hour and latitude (§6.2) |
| RecordFact, Communicate `message` | the fact, who knows it | story facts | kept in code and injected as [KNOWS] (§6.12) |
| Appraise | `about`, `matters`, good/bad, `wants_it_for_them`, came true, praiseworthy, result, appealing | the character's view | co-occurring emotion types and intensity words from the OCC model (§6.13) |
| Price | question (goods, daily wage, yearly cost of living, value of English money), item, trade, quantity, city, country, money, year | scene facts | silver and labour-days from Allen 2001 and GPIH (§6.14) |
| NameCharacter | sex, birth year | story facts | first name by US frequency (§6.11) |
| Disease | disease, population, immune share | story facts | incubation and outbreak size (§6.15) |
| Weather | station | scene fact | rain and temperature by WGEN (§6.16) |
| Layout | spots (x, y, floor, room), rooms (corners, floor, height) | the plan | distances, room sizes, rooms of spots, walls between people (§6.20a) |
| Act | who, steps (be_at, walk_to, sit, stand, bend, kneel, reach, grasp…) | durations, clock, spot | MTM-1 times and own walking speed; moves the clock (§6.20) |
| Room | room, volume, air changes, fires | room air | CO, CO2, O2 each minute from fuel atoms and breathing; occupants breathe it (§6.22) ; exit width and pace → time to get everyone out, flow, crushed (§6.22) |
| Substance | compound (name or SMILES), temperature | scene fact | state, burning, flash point, fire limits from measured data or structure (§6.19) |
| Choose | options, their shares and kinds | outcome guess, as labelled bands; kind a scene fact | drawn in code, moved by the chooser's traits, fear, anger, drink and body (§6.17) |
| BodySize | country, sex, birth year, story year | story facts | height and mass from NCD-RisC (§6.11) |
| Battle | attackers, defenders, year, place, surprise | story facts | outcome by force ratio, era and surprise; casualties and hours from a matched CDB90 battle (§6.18) |
| (no tool) | persuasion beyond small requests, morale and fleeing, task times, prices, who wins a negotiation | decided by the model | OPEN-89, OPEN-90 |

### 7.2 Defences against known model failures

Published failure modes of language models, and what the engine does about each.

| Failure (source) | Effect in a story | Defence |
|---|---|---|
| Models cave when the user pushes back: asked "Are you sure?", assistants drop correct answers and agree with the user's mistaken belief (Sharma et al. 2024, ICLR; larger and RLHF models more so, Perez et al. 2023) | the player writes "my sword hits" or disputes a miss, and the narrator gives in | brief: a player's message states attempts; results stand. Code: a decision tool (BodyCheck, Listen, Look, Communicate, SocialResponse, HoldOn, Chase, Choose, NewCharacter, NameCharacter, BodySize, Disease, Weather) called again in the same turn for the same actor and act returns its first result ("Already decided this turn"), like the RollTheDice ledger. A real second attempt names it ("pick lock, 2nd try"). Swipes re-run the turn from its snapshot with the same seed. |
| Models cannot produce random sequences: over-alternation, aversion to long runs, first-flip bias (Van Koevering & Kleinberg 2024) | "random" picks by the narrator are biased and steerable | all draws come from the seeded code RNG; brief: random picks (which door, coin, card) → RollTheDice table |
| Instructions fade over turns: significant drift within eight rounds (Li et al. 2024, COLM) | rules followed early, ignored later | the rules brief is re-injected every turn at the end of the context, not only in the system prompt |
| Information in the middle of a long context is used worst (U-shaped curve; Liu et al. 2024, TACL) | rules or state buried mid-context are ignored | brief and tracker state go in the per-turn injection near the end |
| A model commits to an early wrong answer and then justifies it (Zhang et al. 2023, hallucination snowballing) | narration states an outcome, then the tool is called to fit it | every tool says "before narrating"; the audit pass flags outcomes narrated without a tool call, now including the hearing, sight, message, lie, bystander, order, request, grip, chase, first-meeting and contact-burn tools, where the scene leaves the outcome in doubt. Its answer belongs to the swipe it read: a swipe shown while it ran (of the same words) gets no flags and no rewrite, and an answer that comes after the player wrote on stays on its reply and rewrites nothing (2026-10-26 (98), R29) |
| Irrelevant detail in the context lowers accuracy (Shi et al. 2023, ICML, "Large Language Models Can Be Easily Distracted by Irrelevant Context") | chances, intermediate values and citations in tool results anchor the narration ("only 31 %…") or leak into the story | tool results reach the narrator as the outcome plus its cause in words, never a chance or a source: BodyCheck says "SUCCESS, despite reduced grasp" or "FAILURE, because of no working grasp", and a failure with no limiting condition says so ("an ordinary failure: no injury or condition caused it"), since an outcome without a cause invites the narrator to invent one. Remaining parentheses holding a %, a year, SII, "chance" or a level in dB, and status tags ([ASSUMED …], [estimated]), are removed as a safety net (`plain`), and the sensitivity note ("Fragile: …") stays out of both ways a result reaches the narrator, the [DECIDED] block and its own tool calls (`storyText`); the full text goes to the browser console. Data tables stay in code. |
| Tool-calling models misfire with many tools and fail to detect irrelevant ones (Patil et al. 2025, BFCL) | the wrong tool, or BodyCheck for a task a specialist tool decides | BodyCheck refuses tasks whose words match a specialist tool and names it (§7.1) |
| A token in the context raises its own chance of being written (induction heads copy the token that followed an earlier occurrence: Olsson et al. 2022, arXiv 2209.11895); negated prompts get worse with scale (Jang et al. 2022, arXiv 2209.12711) | a list of measured modern clothes, the survey's acts, or "elves, goblins" in a brief for real history pulls those words into an 1850 scene | each model reads the tools as the scene allows them (`scene-fit.ts`): options, arguments and tools carry the years they exist and the worlds they belong to, and the examples name only what any scene holds or a placeholder the scene fills ("<place>: <part>") |
| Models pick options by position or label rather than content (selection bias; Zheng et al. 2024, ICLR) | the narrator keeps choosing the first band | the bands carry their meaning in their text, not a letter ID (removing IDs cut the bias most in Zheng et al.), and are reshuffled before every generation: one fixed shuffle per load keeps a fixed position bias |
| A model judging two answers favours one by position; asking it to give its evidence first reduces that (Multiple Evidence Calibration; Wang et al. 2024, ACL); forced formats can cut reasoning quality (Tam et al. 2024) | the check pass misses or invents unbacked events | the check pass writes its evidence as plain lines first and the JSON list of events after; code reads only the JSON. Free reasoning first and format extraction second kept reasoning quality in Tam et al. 2024 (EMNLP Industry), and the check runs as a fresh call that has not seen the narrator's reasoning (Zhang et al. 2023: a model defends its own earlier answer) |
| Irrelevant, specific detail in the context lowers accuracy (Shi et al. 2023) | a fight scene pulls London bread prices into the prompt because they sat in the body lorebook entry | every set is kept at one level: the rules brief has one routing line per kind of event, grouped Body, Surroundings, Senses, People, World, Principles, with scopes (cities, years, settings) only in the tool descriptions; the lorebook is split by topic with its own trigger words (576 body, 578 senses and light, 579 people, 580 prices and disease); an enum lists one kind of thing (Price: question, goods, trade as separate enums) |
| Function-calling accuracy drops as the number of functions grows (Patil et al. 2025, BFCL; BFCL does not test merging tools); RPG-MCP cut 195 tools to 36 by merging them behind action enums, and SkillComposer reports the same consolidation | a narrow tool sits beside general ones and is missed or misused | the five social tools (lie, emergency, order, favour, first meeting) are one tool, SocialResponse, with the situation as an enum (28 → 24 tools); sound and noise presets are loudness classes with the measured example in parentheses, so a horse cart or a forge maps to a class instead of a modern machine standing out in a period scene |

### 7.3 Question pass

Before each reply to a player message, the State Model (the connection the tracker and the check pass use) reads the player's message, the last 8 messages and [KNOWS], with every rule tool listed by its arguments (enums of up to 40 values shown, longer ones as free text [ASSUMED]). It writes one question per outcome in doubt, then a JSON list of tool calls, at most 8 [ASSUMED]. Code drops calls to unknown tools or with a required argument missing, runs the rest in order through the same guarded action the narrator's calls use, and gives the narrator the results in a [DECIDED] block. The check pass counts them as engine results of the turn. A narrator call that repeats one of them returns the same result ("Already decided this turn").

**The player's line, read back and asked about (2026-10-23 (10), (11), (12), (13); `reading.ts`, `ask.ts`, `src/ui.ts`, `style.css`, `fonts/`).** Prose is the player's; the choices that change an outcome are made exact, as a compiler takes any style and stops only at an ambiguous call. Under each player line a note shows what code took from it, one row per call for the player's character: a tag naming the kind of rule (Sight, Sound, Speech, Body, Move, Mind, Time, Thing; `reading.ts` kindOf) in that kind's colour, then the call in the fewest words that decide it, the player's character left out since it makes every call shown ("Sight Tom · 15 m", "Speech whisper → the guard: 'Open the gate'", "Body vault the low wall · 46 %"); the things read are Thing rows ("minigun → M134"). Hovering a row shows its call, tool and arguments, as Disco Elysium keeps a check's modifiers in its tooltip. The note is an aside in a book serif and italics, folded to one line (the guess, then each tag and its words, cut with an ellipsis) and opened by a click to a row per call; the player chose these (2026-10-23: the most information for the words and the space, book serif italic, one colour per kind, folded, prose questions, period type). Each row keeps its plain sentence too (`readingOf`: "Ann looks at the rider, 40 m away", a paraphrase as Attempto Controlled English shows its reading), shown for a note kept before the tags. The note stays under the line (kept on the message, `extra.rulesReading`, and drawn whenever the chat is drawn): a note about one message belongs next to it (Nielsen Norman Group, "Indicators, Validations, and Notifications"), and SillyTavern's toasts close after 4 s at the top of the screen (`public/script.js` toastr options), too soon for the four lines and "Taken as" with no other place to read them (WCAG 2.2 SC 2.2.1, Understanding document: a message that vanishes with no other way to get it is a time limit). Inform prints its parser's choice the same way, in parentheses in the transcript ("(the green box)"). The note is drawn in the user's theme: SillyTavern's theme variables (`--SmartThemeEmColor`, `--SmartThemeQuoteColor`, `--SmartThemeUnderlineColor`, `--SmartThemeBodyColor`, `--mainFontSize`), after its reasoning block's left rule (`.mes_reasoning`); every word is set as text. The kind colours are Okabe & Ito's colour-blind-safe hues (Color Universal Design, 2008), each mixed with the theme's text colour by a share set per tone (light text: a dark theme, or dark text) so that it reads at 4.5:1 or more (WCAG 1.4.3) on every theme SillyTavern ships (Azure, Cappuccino, Celestial Macaron, Dark Lite, Dark V 1.0: text, chat tint and message tint over black) and on a light one; colour never carries the kind alone, the tag's word does too. Period type follows the story's year (`ui.ts` eraOf; `rules-tools.ts` storyYearNow), each face OFL-licensed and subset to Latin (`fonts/`): before 1600 EB Garamond (Garamont's roman and Granjon's italic from the Berner specimen, 1592); 1600–1749 IM FELL English (the Fell types of the Oxford press, cut in the 1670s–80s); 1750–1799 Baskerville (designed 1757, Libre Baskerville's subset renamed, as the OFL asks of a modified font with a Reserved Font Name); 1800–1919 Old Standard (the Modern, Didone, style that came to dominate printing by the mid-nineteenth century, as late-nineteenth-century editions used it); 1920–1969 Libre Caslon Text (Caslon as mid-twentieth-century lettering drew it; its italic's s–p and s–t ligatures read as a long s, so standard ligatures are off); from 1970, or with no calendar date, Georgia and the system's book serifs. Old-style figures where the face has them; one x-height across faces (`font-size-adjust`); the guess is marked by the manicule (☞) up to 1919 and an arrow after. A line edited since its reading shows no note (the note keeps the words it read). What a line leaves open: a verb whose senses go to different tools (follow someone: catch up, keep behind them out of sight, walk after them openly; take or hold a bridge, a gate, a hill: a fight for it, or going onto it) [ASSUMED list], or "him"/"her" with no one named before it, or with two or more named before it who fit ("I look at Tom and Will, then I punch him": one of them, asked between them; 2026-10-26 (102), before nothing asked and the planner chose; one named who fits is that one, as frame.ts reads it), or a blow with no one named at all (FrameNet's Cause_harm with its Victim left out: "I strike!"), with two or more people in the recent scene it fits (people of the pronoun's sex first; "them" is a group and is not asked). Whether to ask follows the mixed-initiative rule (Horvitz 1999, CHI): act on the likeliest reading when its share of the weight reaches a threshold that rises with the cost of a wrong act, 0.9 when a reading harms someone (a blow, a battle) and 0.5 otherwise [ASSUMED values, registered with ranges]; ask when it does not, or on a tie. The weights rank readings as interactive-fiction parsers rank the objects a word may mean (Inform's "does the player mean" rules; Hugo's +1 for the object last referred to): 1 each, +2 for the person the player acted on last turn, +1 for one the narrator's last message names, +1 for one at the player's spot; for a verb's senses the plain one 2 (walk after; go onto the bridge), the others 1 (the fight 0.2), +3 for a sense the scene shows (someone fleeing: the chase; soldiers about: the fight; stealth words: out of sight) [ASSUMED weights, registered]. A reading taken unasked leads the note, folded or not ("☞ walk after the thief in the open — or…"): implicit confirmation, the cheaper grounding (Clark & Brennan 1991); a question is explicit confirmation. Opened, on the player's latest line only, the note offers the readings passed over as linked words ("Or did you mean to catch up with the thief or keep behind the thief, out of sight?"), each putting its correction in the input box ("no, catch up with the thief"), the player's own next line, read by the correction rule below; one action per such message ("Each snackbar may contain a single action", Material Design 1, Snackbars & toasts), and a wrong guess is undone rather than warned about beforehand (Raskin, "Never Use a Warning When You Mean Undo"). The turn keeps the readings it passed over (`asked.taken`), so a swipe of it still offers them. Asked, the options share one form, carry no default and come in a fresh order (Zheng et al. 2024): the popup quotes the player's words, then asks in one sentence with every choice a linked phrase of the same form, numbered (number keys pick), the words the choices share said once ("Do you mean to hit ¹Tom or ²Will?"), in the story's period type; no buttons, and the sentence, not a choice, holds the focus, so Enter (the key that just sent the line) picks nothing. SillyTavern's confirm popup, used before for two options, coloured its first button and focused it (`popup.css` `.popup-button-ok`; `popup.js` setAutoFocus), a default, and defaults are picked more often (Johnson & Goldstein 2003, Science 302:1338; replicated by Chandrashekar et al. 2023). The answer is fixed for the turn (required of the plan, read by the planner as THE PLAYER'S CHOICE, set on the player's call); "Say it another way", closing the popup or Escape stops the reply so the player says it another way. The two-things question is drawn the same way, each thing's description after it in italics ("Do you mean ¹Argand lamp (1780) (an oil lamp with a hollow round wick…) or ²oil lamp (…)?"). With reduced motion asked for, the popup opens without animation. A correction in the next line ("no, Will"; "I meant to keep out of sight"; Elgohary et al. 2020 for plain-word corrections of a parse) is read when short, unquoted and naming a person or a sense the last line could have meant: the world goes back to the snapshot taken before that turn, its line is planned again with the correction fixed on the scene as it stood (the reply that told the wrong version is left out of what the planner reads), its record of changes is cut back, and the narrator is told the last reply's account did not happen. The redoing turn starts from that restored world too, and keeps its correction: a swipe of its reply goes back to the restored world (before, it went back to the world the undone turn left, and the undone blow stood again) and tells the narrator again that the last account did not happen (before, the swipe dropped it).
