The note and the popup are checked with a stand-in document (tests/ui.test.mjs) and were drawn in Chromium with SillyTavern's own stylesheets, in each era, dark and light [not yet seen inside SillyTavern]. Check: N60 (asked, picked, fixed), N61 (what is asked), N62 (the reading), N63 (when to ask: a look at "him" takes Tom named last, a blow asks; a quiet bridge is walked onto, a held one asked; a bolting thief chased, a strolling one walked after; a bare "I strike!" asked), N64 (a correction redoes the turn: the record holds the blow at Will only), N66 (a swipe of the redone turn keeps Tom unstruck and the narrator told), N67 (a taken reading keeps the others, on a swipe too; its change line redoes the turn), tests/ui.test.mjs (tagged rows without the actor; the guess and the others on the latest line only; the folded line; drawn as text; kept on player lines; the question as one sentence with alike numbered choices, no default, clicks and number keys; every change line read back as its reading; the era bands, mark and tone; every kind colour at 4.5:1 on SillyTavern's themes and a light one; the period faces present, licensed and not named with a Reserved Font Name).

**How a line is said (2026-10-23 (14), (15); `prosody.ts`, `voice-code.ts`, the voice toolbar in `src/ui.ts`).** Speech is more than its words: pitch, stress, pauses, breath, tempo and voice quality carry how it is meant, and a line's words alone leave them to the narrator's guess. The player marks them without signs (2026-10-23 (15), the player's pick: an improved look with the backend kept whole; `voice-code.ts`): selecting words in the message box opens a toolbar of plain words above it, each set in the effect it names ("louder" in small capitals, "rises" lifted, "slower" spaced; groups Pitch, Volume, Pace, Voice, Breath, Weight, Feeling, each with one soft colour), and the ♪ button beside the box opens it for the word at the cursor; picking one again takes it off. The pick is kept unseen in the line: each marked word carries a block of Unicode tag characters right after its last letter (U+E0020–U+E007E, the printable ASCII mirrored, closed by U+E007F CANCEL TAG, the block emoji subdivision flags use; Wikipedia, "Tags (Unicode block)"), which take no width and show nothing (checked in Chromium: the same width with and without them; the cursor steps over a letter and its marks as one). Backspace and Delete take a marked letter as one character and leave its marks on the word's next letter, or take them too when the word is left with no letter or digit, whatever punctuation stays ("I—", "I…", "«I»"; before, an invisible block was left on the dash) (Chromium alone takes one invisible character per key press). A layer over the box (clicks pass through it), the box's text invisible in the same type and place, underlines each marked word in its group's soft colour; sent, the line is typeset in the chat the way it sounds (louder in small capitals, rising lifted, slower spaced, softer smaller, breathy blurred, creaky with a wavy rule, a pause as a faint dot or dash, "inhales" and the feeling as small words; hover names the marks). Before a model reads the chat, every player's line with tag characters is rewritten in the notation below and the tag characters are gone (invisible text in a player's line, a known way to slip instructions to a model unseen, never reaches one); the saved chat keeps the marks hidden. Every way out to a model takes out any character of the Tags block left (U+E0000–U+E007F; `voice-code.ts` untagged): the engine's own requests (`send.ts`), the narrator's prompt and the tools' results, so one in a card, a fact or a reply goes no further either (2026-10-26 (102); engine R54, ui R62). Typed by hand, the notation reads the same. The marks are conversation analysis's (Jefferson's transcription conventions, as the CA appendices after Atkinson & Heritage 1984 give them): (.) a micropause under 0.2 s, (0.5) a timed pause, ↑ ↓ a marked shift of pitch, CAPITALS louder than the speaker's surrounding talk, °…° distinctly quieter, wo::rd a prolonged sound, wor- an abrupt cut-off, hhh an out-breath and .hhh an in-breath, wo(h)rd laughter within a word, ((…)) a description. Three are written otherwise so SillyTavern keeps them: › ‹ faster and ‹ › slower (Jefferson's > < would be read as HTML), ☺…☺ smile voice (the Danish CA conventions' sign; $ and £ are currencies), and the IPA's ˈ before a stressed word (underlining does not survive plain text; * and _ already mean actions and italics). Voice quality is VoQS's (Ball, Esling & Dickson 1995): {V̤ … V̤} breathy, {V̰ … V̰} creaky, {W … W} whisper, {F … F} falsetto, each label written apart from the words it brackets; quotation marks, brackets, dashes and ellipses may cling to a brace ('"Wait—{W not now W},"'; before, a dash before the opener left "{W" among the words), a label glued to a word is no span ("{WhelloW}", as "{Fred}" is none). Code reads every player's line from its words, the toolbar's codes or the signs typed, never from the notation made for the models (before, earlier lines and a written time were read from it: a pause marked on "2:20 pm" set the clock to 2:20 at night, and "again" repeated "sho::ve"; R28). Text without marks reads as before: currency, times, a temperature (a closing sign closes only a span opened before it), hyphenated words, a colon before speech, single parentheses and capitals that are abbreviations (a list [ASSUMED]; one it lacks reads as loud, and the note shows it). Capitals spelling a pronoun, a conjunction or an auxiliary after a determiner are an abbreviation too, since pronouns do not permit determiners (CGEL ch. 5 §10.2, p. 429): "the WHO", "the IT desk", "the OR"; without one they read as louder ("WHO did this?"), so "in IT" still reads as a louder "it" and the note shows it (2026-10-26 (99)). AIDS and SIM are listed (dictionary nouns written in capitals: Merriam-Webster, "AIDS"; "SIM card", variant "SIM"); PIN, SWAT and LED, nouns that also spell a verb said louder, stay as written after a determiner or a possessive ("my PIN", "the SWAT team", "an LED lamp"; Oxford Learner's Dictionaries, "PIN"; Merriam-Webster, "SWAT", "LED") and read as louder without one ("PIN him down!"); RAM stays louder ("RAM the gate!"). A hyphen before a colon is a face drawn in letters (D-:), not a cut-off (2026-10-26 (101); ui R49). The typesetting's added words (inhales, angrily, a pause's dot) are not selected with the line, so a line copied from the chat is the player's own; a pause's title gives its own length. What the marks fix, in code: the line's words without them are what the verbs, names and things are read from ("pu::nch Tom" is a blow); the player's Communicate takes the effort they give (every spoken word whispered: whisper; every spoken word in capitals: loud; some: raised [ASSUMED mapping: Jefferson's capitals are relative loudness]) and the feeling ((angry)) names, whatever the plan wrote; the planner reads the delivery as fixed, the narrator is told it in [DECIDED] ("How Jack said the line: feeling: anger · louder: Stop · pitch up on think · short pause after me"), and the note under the line shows it as its first row, tagged Voice. A feeling in the voice is heard right as often as listeners hear it: Juslin & Laukka 2003 (Psychol Bull 129:770), Table 4, unweighted mean pi within one culture and across cultures (anger .93/.91, fear .88/.82, happiness .87/.74, sadness .93/.91, tenderness .82/.71; tenderness across cultures rests on one study), read as the share right among the five feelings by Rosenthal & Rubin's pi solved for p, p = pi / (4 − 3 pi) (anger within one culture 76.9 %, happiness across cultures 41.6 %); across cultures when speaker and listener share no language they are known to speak; a miss is one of the other four, each as likely [ASSUMED: the meta-analysis gives accuracy, not the confusions]; only a voice that is heard carries it ("Tom hears Ann…; in the voice Tom hears fear (Ann meant anger)"). Open: what pitch, pauses, breath and voice quality change beyond the feeling (whether a pause reads as nerves or a lie, a breathy voice as intimacy) waits for a source with rates. Check: tests/ui.test.mjs (every mark read, ordinary text unread, each toolbar choice marking its word unseen and read back, on and off, the words shown unchanged; a selection marking every word; Backspace taking a marked letter whole; the box layer holding the box's text exactly; the chat's typesetting; the notation a model reads; the feeling rates; the Voice row), N70 (marks kept unseen fix the delivery as typed ones do), N68 (heard right 62.6 % within one culture and 41.6 % across, none when the voice is not heard), N69 (capitals raise the voice and ((angry)) sets the feeling on the player's call; planner and narrator told; a stretched verb still read).

| Step | Source |
|---|---|
| questions answered apart from the draft, before writing | Chain-of-Verification (Dhuliawala et al. 2023, arXiv 2309.11495): factored verification beat joint; list precision 0.17 → 0.36, biography FactScore 55.9 → 71.4 (LLaMA 65B); a model defends an outcome it has already written (Zhang et al. 2023) |
| ask a question when the next sentence is uncertain, answer it from a source, then write | FLARE (Jiang et al. 2023, arXiv 2305.06983), with the rule engine as the source |
| planning agents ahead of the writing agent | Agents' Room (Huot et al. 2024) |
| free-text questions first, JSON after | Tam et al. 2024 (as the check pass) |

A swipe of the same turn reuses the calls asked the first time; the engine state is back at the turn's start, so they return the same outcomes. The pass a line keeps is its own while its words are the same, and the draws follow the line's words and the call count, not its place (the count of lines), which a deletion before the line moves: before 2026-10-26 (102), a swipe after an earlier turn was deleted read a correction afresh (its blow at Will lost) and drew again (engine R51). A correction reads the line before it in SillyTavern's chat, which still holds the reply a swipe rewrites (before, an edited correction swiped corrected itself). Whenever the pass runs a turn's calls again it first puts the world back at the line's start itself (2026-10-26 (98), `rules-tools.ts` restoreLineStart): since 2026-10-26 (100), the start kept when the line's first pass ran (below); it was a snapshot by chat position, which a snapshot taken after the pass had run could stand in for. That covers a swipe, a regenerate, and Send with the box empty after a reply failed, was stopped or was deleted (SillyTavern adds no message then and starts a 'normal' generation: before, the turn's 20 minutes became 40); a line edited in place after its pass runs as edited, from the same start. After the narrator's own tool calls SillyTavern saves them as a message and starts a 'normal' generation for the reply (`script.js` Generate): a swipe of that reply keeps the tools' effects while function calling is on (the tool message stays in the prompt and the pass does not run), and with function calling off the tool message leaves the prompt (`coreChat` keeps a system message only while `isToolCallingSupported()`), the pass's chat ends at the player's line, and the turn is redone from the line's start (before, its blow landed twice). A new line sent after a stopped reply keeps the stopped line's outcome: that line stays in the chat. Check: R26, R27.

The world over time follows one rule (2026-10-26 (100)): the world is the start, plus each player's line in the chat the outcome its last question pass decided, plus each narrator tool call whose message is in the chat, as the swipes shown have it. To keep it, every message the engine knows holds the world it ended with and how many changes the record held then (`extra.rulesResult`, `extra.rulesLog`): a reply's swipe when it is received, a player's line when its pass has run, a narrator's tool call and the greeting when a generation starts from them. A deletion puts back the world of the last message that holds one, so a line whose reply is deleted keeps its outcome (it stays in the chat, and the narrator reads it), and the record is cut to what it held then. Each player's line keeps the world it starts from when its first pass runs (`extra.rulesStart`, with the call count and the record's count of changes then; a correction's once the world is put back) and its pass (`extra.rulesPass`: its calls, and what a correction meant), so a pass run again, a swipe or a regenerate of its reply, and a correction of it start from the line itself, whichever order it joined in and whatever is deleted before or after it (2026-10-26 (101)); before, the engine kept one start, the latest line's, and snapshots by chat position, which a deletion moves. A swipe of a reply starts from its line's start, or after the narrator's tool calls from the world the last tool message keeps; the record is cut by its count of changes. A chat saved by entry 100 has the engine's `lineStart` for its latest line; else the world the message before the line ended with. Player lines and tool messages more than 30 messages back let go of the worlds, starts and passes they keep; replies keep the world of the swipe shown, for a deletion back to one or a branch from it, so a chat holds one world per reply (before, one per message, and on old replies one per swipe: 2026-10-26 (102), engine R52). Only the last message can be swiped: a swipe back to another swipe of a reply that far back, once the chat is deleted back to it, keeps the world shown. A deletion more than 30 messages back to a player line starts from the reply before it, as the 30 snapshots did before entry 100. After any restore, when the record no longer rebuilds the world, one entry ("restored", "swipe shown") holds the difference. Check: `tests/sequences.test.mjs` plays random chats through SillyTavern's own event order (Send, an empty Send, a stop, `/send` then `/trigger`, regenerate, swipe, swipe back, deleting the last messages, an edit, the narrator's PassTime and RecordFact calls with function calling on or off) and checks the clock, the facts and the record against the rule after every step: 300 sequences of 24 steps in `npm test`, and 6,000 of 40 steps on six seeds, with no failing sequence. Run on entry 99's tree, the same 300 found 206 failing sequences; the classes and one case each are R36–R41. The cases a fourth review found, and the ones its checks led to, are R42–R44 and R46: a correction after a later line is deleted, at the start of its reply's swipe, and after an earlier turn is deleted; a swipe, a swipe back and a new swipe after a deletion in the middle of the chat (630, not 620); a line whose pass failed after its calls ran, sent again (607, not 614). Not covered by the sequences: a deletion in the middle of the chat (the later replies were written on the world as it was, so it stands; R44 has its cases), `/rules-time` between turns, function calling switched mid-chat, and corrections. A line `/send` puts in before `/trigger` joins the chat before the generation starts, so its start sits at the reply's place: a regenerate of it starts from the line's start (R33), a correction sent that way keeps the restored world as its start (before, a stopped correction's regenerate brought the undone blow back; R32), and a deleted line takes its pass with it, so a new line in its place is a new turn and `/rules-time` set in between stands (R34). Setting: "Rules question pass", on by default with the rules engine. Live check (§11): the pass runs before the narrator request, a narrator BodyCheck repeating the pass's BodyCheck returned "Already decided this turn", an unknown tool name was dropped, and the [DECIDED] block reached the narrator and the check pass.

IF the question pass ran this turn (also with no calls) → THEN the narrator gets RULES_BRIEF_DECIDED and [DECIDED] and no tool schemas (`shouldRegister`); an action [DECIDED] leaves open is narrated up to the moment its outcome would show. IF the pass failed or is off → THEN the narrator gets RULES_BRIEF and the 28 tools. Process: the 28 schemas measure 42,998 characters (≈ 12,300 tokens at 3.5 characters per token), and each narrator tool call adds a second full request; a live run on NVIDIA z-ai/glm-5.3 timed out at ≈ 4 min with them and answered a 13-token test in under 1 min.

IF an outcome follows from another outcome still in doubt (a blow after an order is obeyed, an answer after a call is heard) → THEN the question pass lists it under "then"; code runs the first calls, sends their results back once, and runs the calls written for the follow-on questions the results leave open (at most one extra round and 8 calls per turn; decide, read the result, decide again: FLARE, Jiang et al. 2023; ReAct, Yao et al. 2023). IF STORY STATE has no [TIME] and the scene, cast or scenario gives a date or hour → THEN the question pass returns it as "time" and code sets the story clock; a swipe of the same turn sets it again. IF the reply covers more than a few minutes → THEN PassTime. IF an undescribed person acts or speaks in the reply → THEN NewCharacter, once per person (the narrator would otherwise invent their sex, age and trade); the scorer counts it like RecordFact, never as extra. IF a feeling or a pick belongs to the player's character → THEN it comes from the player: Appraise and Choose are for the other characters. Process: GLM-5.3 on `/rules-eval 10` (2026-10-12) scored, after these rules, precision 58.8 %, recall 100 %, deciding arguments 100 %, quiet 100 %; 6 of its 7 extra calls were NewCharacter for people who act in the scene, 1 an Appraise. Before them it scored recall 90 %, deciding arguments 100 %, quiet 100 %, precision 50 %; the 10 extra calls were follow-on outcomes (BodyStrike after an order, SocialResponse after a call, Choose after a kick), PassTime for under two minutes, NewCharacter for background figures, and RollTheDice with no table; two BodyStrike calls failed on `part` being required while its description said to omit it (now optional: the location is drawn).

**Player claims.** A player writes plain language, so what a message states about their own character is a claim, not a fact; the world comes from the scenario and the world's facts, never from a claim. IF the kind of thing claimed does not exist in the world → THEN it does not hold, with no draw. IF a message states something the story has not established about the player's character (what they are, an item they have, something they can do, a past event) → THEN Claim: a reference class and its base share for the setting, an estimate for this story, combined in log-odds as in BodyCheck, then a draw. IF it holds → THEN it is recorded as the character's own fact; a claimed creature (DF creature id) becomes the character's body when no wound is on record. IF it does not hold → THEN "Not so: …" is recorded and the narrator shows the attempt meeting the world as it is. Either way it stays: a repeat in the same turn returns the same result and [KNOWS] carries it to later turns. Source of the rule: "say yes or roll the dice" (Baker 2004, Dogs in the Vineyard): an uncontested claim passes, a contested one is decided by chance. A creature's body then decides the rest: a cat that is a cat makes cat sounds (Communicate, CAN_SPEAK). Scenes 51–53 score it.

**Applied once, in its own chat (2026-10-27 (101); `question-pass.ts`, `rules-tools.ts`, `rules-audit.ts`, `audit-flags.ts`).**
IF a question pass fails after some of its calls ran (a later model call refused, a rate limit after its retries) → THEN the line's start is put back (`restoreLineStart`) and the narrator's own tool calls decide the turn, so no call runs twice. Check: PassTime 60 then a failed follow-on round leaves the clock where it was (engine "a pass that fails after its calls ran takes them back").
IF a line edited in place becomes a correction ("I wait 5 minutes." → "no, Will") → THEN the world the correction put back is the line's start (`keepStart(i, true)`), so a swipe of its reply starts there. Check: Tom 0, Will 1 after the swipe.
IF another chat is opened while a tool waits (the sky, the weather, a bleed, chemistry data) → THEN the call decides nothing and writes nothing (`run` throws when the chat is no longer the call's). Check: the other chat holds no `rulesEngine` after the wait.
IF the rule check's redo never starts → THEN its correction note and flags go to no later turn or other chat (both carry the turn key). Check: `takeAuditNote()` in another chat returns null.

**Ordinary writing stays words (2026-10-27 (101); `prosody.ts`, `frame.ts`, `router.ts`, `intent.ts`, `ask.ts`, `voice-code.ts`).**
IF a whole number stands in brackets ("Tom (35)", "the year (1850)") → THEN it is text; a timed pause carries its decimal point, (0.5), (2.0) (Jefferson's pauses are timed in tenths of a second). IF a degree sign comes before C, F or K ("20 °C") → THEN it is a unit, no quieter span. IF a verb of falling takes an object or a predicative complement ("drop the sword", "trip the guard", "fall asleep") → THEN it routes no BodyFall. IF "with …" or "about …" follows the one spoken to → THEN it is a manner or a topic, no message. IF "him" or "her" follows a person of the other sex named in the line → THEN "back" still reads the one who struck. IF a line quotes in any quotation marks → THEN it is no correction. Emphasis inside emphasis ("*I punch **Tom**.*") is read as its words. A mark on a word in guillemets sits on the letters («Stop-»). A pronoun looked at is the person the line names before it.

**Verbs by the sense their line gives, and the world kept per reply (2026-10-27 (102); `router.ts`, `grammar.ts`, `frame.ts`, `prosody.ts`, `voice-code.ts`, `json-text.ts`, `rules-tools.ts`, `index.ts`).**
IF a weather verb's subject is not "it" ("I pour Tom a drink", "I blow out the candle") → THEN no Weather (Levin 1993 §57). IF a verb is one of Levin's verbs of killing by a means (§42.2, VerbNet poison-42.2) → THEN its means names the tool: poison Substance, drown BodyEnvironment, strangle, throttle, smother, suffocate, asphyxiate and garrotte BodyChoke, stab and knife BodyStrike. IF VerbNet has a phrasal entry (pick_up) → THEN that entry's sense: "pick up the lantern" is Transfer, no eating. "Take a seat" is Act, "break the silence" Communicate, "force a smile" no effort, "sneak, slip, creep or steal past someone" Act and Look (the one passed the viewer). IF a weapon or a limb is swung or thrust at a person or animal → THEN BodyStrike (Levin §1.3, the conative "at"). IF a hand-over counts in figures ("give Will 3 coins") → THEN the quantity is that number. IF a posture is held for a timed while ("sit by the fire for an hour") → THEN PassTime for that while. IF a clause with its own pronoun subject is joined on after a comma ("ask Tom for the key, and he gives it to me") → THEN the words said end before it. A name the story holds keeps its capital in a line shouted in capitals. Every request to a model leaves with hidden marks as notation (SillyTavern's CHAT_COMPLETION_SETTINGS_READY and TEXT_COMPLETION_SETTINGS_READY carry each one, the summary's raw prompt among them).
The world each message ended with is kept in the chat's archive (`rulesEngine.worlds`) as the changes from the world kept before it, whole every 50; a message holds its world's id (`extra.rulesWorld`), and worlds no message reaches go. Check: 120 scripted turns, chat 1.09 → 0.20 MB, metadata 0.44 → 0.64 MB; a world after a mid-chat deletion, a deletion back to reply 20 and a branch at reply 12 equal the worlds kept then.

**A thing's load, someone else's feeling, stealth, and the narrator's acts (2026-10-28 (110); `router.ts`, `question-pass.ts`, `rules-tools.ts`, `eval.ts`).**
IF a thing is the subject of bear, support or withstand, or of hold, take or carry with a weight or a person or no object, or gives way (not "to" someone) → THEN Break (its description: "a shelf, a rope someone hangs from"): "does the shelf hold Mara's weight", "will the plank hold", "the floor gives way"; "the box holds three apples" is containing, "Mara gives way to the duke" yields.
IF a verb of feeling (data/activity-verbs.ts EMOTION_VERBS) has a third person as subject in a complement clause or an inverted question → THEN SocialResponse joins Appraise (their response to the scene): "whether Elias liked her", "does the guard like me"; the player's own character is no third person ("How does Mara feel about her brother?": Appraise alone; routeTurn now takes the player's name).
IF the verb is tiptoe, creep or prowl → THEN BodyCheck's sneaking with the motion (Merriam-Webster: tiptoe "to walk or proceed quietly or cautiously", creep 2b "to go timidly, cautiously, or quietly so as to escape notice", prowl "to move about or wander stealthily"; WordNet's definitions leave the stealth out).
IF a put verb moves a thing (no body part) into, in or under the actor's own container or garment (WordNet kinds n, r) → THEN Transfer, the thing taken ("slid it into her sleeve", "tuck the letter into my coat"; "put my hand in my pocket" is none).
IF the narrator's last messages route a recording tool (Transfer, BodyWound, BodyIntake, Dose, Trust: "record ... the moment it happens", "once the story has it happen") → THEN the planner is offered it; a narrated Transfer counts only by giving, stealing, robbing or seizing (VerbNet give-13.1, contribute-13.2, steal-10.5, rob-10.6, obtain-13.5.2; hand, pocket, lend, borrow, snatch) or into one's own things, of a physical thing ("finds a spring", "learned bleeding control", "reach the camp" offer none).
IF "how much" or "how many" is fronted before a verb of price or getting → THEN the question is a price (Price), no attack ("How much does the ferryman charge?": the object is the fronted amount, CGEL ch. 10).
A complement clause's "whether" or "that" opens a subject ("whether the baker trusts strangers"); reading to a group is speech ("to the crowd": WordNet's noun.group).
Check (`tests/route.test.mjs`, 8 labelled lines and a check of the player's own feeling and the narration's acts; `tests/engine.test.mjs`: a narrated theft shows Transfer to the planner in full, in play and in the eval).

**The scene's words and the tools' own descriptions route the turn (2026-10-27 (109); `router.ts`, `cue-tools.ts`, `senses.ts`, `verbs.ts`, `grammar.ts`, `data/cue-words.ts`, `data/activity-verbs.ts`, `data/senses.ts`, `question-pass.ts`, `rules-tools.ts`, `eval.ts`).**
IF the router routes a line → THEN the planner sees only those tools in full (the others by one line, reachable through "need"), while the picker the router replaces reads the line and the two messages before it and is told to include a tool when unsure. So the words of those three messages add the tools whose inputs they name (`cue-tools.ts`): a sound (Listen); a riddle, puzzle or cipher (WorkOut); a person's name (NameCharacter); a communicable disease or a symptom (Disease); burning, smoke, cold, heat, a storm, snow, rain, a desert, a temperature, an altitude from 2,500 m in a sentence naming a height (high-altitude illness begins there: Hackett & Roach 2001), going into water (BodyEnvironment); a boat, a voyage or an operation (Sickness); a stove, an oven or a heated pot the line touches (BodyContact); a feeling (Appraise); someone on watch, or gravel or sand underfoot, while the line moves (Listen, Look); someone who spotted the runner or shouted, while the line runs (Chase; running is a verb whose first WordNet sense is moving very fast or hurriedly, or fleeing) [ASSUMED cue lists for the last two]. Nouns: the lexicon's nouns whose first common WordNet sense (no instance, no genus) lies under the tool's anchor synsets; adjectives: WordNet's "similar" satellites of cold and hot whose first or second sense is one. A word in a verb's place (after a subject pronoun, before a determiner), a measure ("a pat of butter") or a name (capitalised inside a sentence: "Flame Lash (Fire, complexity 3)") is no cue; fire opened, returned or held is shooting; cold is the illness only as "a cold".
IF a verb names an activity a tool's description names → THEN that tool joins the verb's own (`data/activity-verbs.ts`: each activity a WordNet synset chosen by its definition, with its troponyms; a verb is listed when its first sense lies there): sneak, slink, skulk, lurk, ride, gallop, mend, repair, fix, sew, forge, carve, knit, jump, leap, swim, pry, balance (BodyCheck); solve, figure out, decipher, decode, calculate, compute, devise, deduce, infer (WorkOut); flee, escape, abscond (Chase). A craft whose object is a body part is none ("fix my hair").
IF a verb of VerbNet's admire-31.2 or marvel-31.3 (Levin 1993 §31.2–31.3: the subject feels) has its first WordNet sense in verb.emotion and the line keeps that sense → THEN Appraise ("I fear the dark", "Tom loves his sister"; "miss", "bear", "support" are other acts first).
IF a noun phrase opens a clause (a determiner or a possessor and up to three words, the last a common noun) → THEN the finite verb after it is read (CGEL ch. 4 §1): "a shard slices her palm", "what a mason earns", "the dog chases the fox", "does the guard hear" (after an inverted auxiliary, the plain form); a verb joined by "and" or "then" shares its subject. IF that subject is a thing (no person, animal or organization first; Levin 1993 §3.3, the instrument subject) → THEN a blow is an accident's wound (BodyWound), and it makes no fall, act, effort, chase, speech or perception.
IF a verb is one of VerbNet's perception classes → THEN Look only for the verbs of seeing (`verbs.ts NON_VISUAL` holds hearing, smelling, tasting, feeling, sensing); notice, detect, perceive and sense offer Look and Listen (FrameNet 1.7's Becoming_aware has no Body_part element, the sense organ Perception_experience has); feeling about or toward something, feeling guilty about, "how does Mara feel" → Appraise; feel, smell, sniff and taste alone → no tool.
VerbNet's roles of amount (Value, Extent, Duration, Asset) left unrestricted take a quantity, money or a time ("carry the chest" is no cost; VerbNet's role definitions, Bonial et al. 2011); a two-object frame's first object (Recipient, Beneficiary) is checked only on a line with two objects ("carry the boy to the cart" is no cost to the boy); "a couple of seconds" is no measure of goods (its oblique's first sense is a time).
Named constructions, each from a tool's description or a FrameNet frame: trust, distrust, confide, betray, rely on, apologize (Trust); lie to (SocialResponse); hang from or on, lug, tote, haul, carry by its handles (HoldOn); run from or away, catch a person (Chase); catch sight (Look); catch fire (Substance); catch a disease (Disease); inject (Dose); remember or forget to, add up, VerbNet's multiply-108 (WorkOut); faint, swoon, slip, trip, stumble (Claim); suffocate-40.7, as choke (BodyChoke); an attack-60.1 verb on a fort or a hill (Battle); clothes or footwear put on or taken off, getting out of water, swimming (BodyEnvironment); standing, jumping or hanging on a plank, beam, shelf, rope, bar or chain (Break); "what does the guard do", "which road does Tom take", "fight or flee" (Choose); reading only aloud or to someone (Communicate); a watched person (Choose, SocialResponse); a quoted or told first-person account in the past or denied (SocialResponse: 27 % of 1,535 diary lies were about the liar's actions, plans and whereabouts; DePaulo, Kashy, Kirkendol, Wyer & Epstein 1996, Table 5; feelings, 30–37 %, left out); persuasion only of a person ("press my palm against the kettle" is touch); the player's character as a kind of being, an item brought out of their clothes or bag, an ability, a past (Claim) [ASSUMED pattern lists].
IF a time follows the object ("I punched Tom two days ago", "I punch Tom this morning") → THEN it is an adjunct, no second object (CGEL ch. 8): since 2026-10-27 (107) the two-object reading had stopped the blow's draft, which hid the time-ago rule (control G46).
Word forms: -es is stripped only after a sibilant or o ("rides" is ride's, not rid's); an irregular past is read as its verb first ("saw" is see's, save after to, a modal or do); an inverted do is the question's auxiliary, no light verb; anyone, someone, everyone are subjects; a particle before a place or a path preposition is a motion's ("bolt down the alley", "paddle out to the buoy"); WordNet's footwear counts as clothing and its roads and paths as places.
Check (`tests/route.test.mjs`, 25 labelled lines and a scene-cue check; `tests/frame.test.mjs`, "I punch Tom this morning"; `tests/engine.test.mjs`): the swim in a freezing lake shows BodyEnvironment to the planner in full; the eval asks the picker only for a line with no verb routed.

**The scene's cues weigh a verb's senses (2026-10-27 (108); `sense-odds.ts`, `data/sense-gloss.ts`, `frame.ts`, `question-pass.ts`).**
Among the senses a verb can still mean (by its object), the readings are the groups of senses that name the same tools. They start equal; each cue multiplies the odds of the readings it fits by its likelihood ratio, and the result is normalized (cue combination, Ernst & Banks 2002; word senses from weighted constraints, MacDonald, Pearlmutter & Seidenberg 1994). Cues: IF the same verb was read earlier in this chat (by the tools its turn called) → THEN that reading's chance becomes `senseOneDiscourse` = 0.94 (Gale, Church & Yarowsky 1992: 51 of 54), or `senseOneCollocation` = 0.95 with the same object (Yarowsky 1993: verb-object pairs .95); IF the actor holds a weapon, gun or projectile (WordNet) → THEN blows and shots × `senseInstrumentLR` = 3 [ASSUMED 1.5–6]; IF a reading's tool was decided in the last six messages → THEN × `senseRecentLR` = 2 [ASSUMED 1.2–4]; IF the scene's words (the line and the two messages before it) are words of a sense's WordNet definition, synonyms, hypernyms or derived nouns (Lesk 1986) → THEN × `senseGlossLR` = 1.5 per word, three at most [ASSUMED 1.1–3]. IF another sense the object fits names another tool → THEN code drafts the blow only when its reading reaches `senseDraftShare` = 0.8 [ASSUMED 0.6–0.95]; the planner is told every verb's readings with their chances and cues (READINGS). Check: "I charge the cook": no cues 25 % each of four readings; a sword 50 %; and a blow just before 67 %; and "attack" said 79 %; an earlier "charge the cook two shillings" Price 95 %. The 461 open phrases of the inventory have 2 readings (196), 3 (219) or 4 (46).

**A verb's sense by what its object is (2026-10-27 (107); `senses.ts`, `data/senses.ts`, `router.ts`, `frame.ts`).**
A verb's VerbNet classes are its senses, and each class states what its direct object must be (its selectional restrictions: fire-10.10, dismissing, takes a person, an animal or an organization; throw-17.1 a physical thing; chew-39.2, as "pick", food). WordNet states what kinds of thing a noun names, from its lexicographer files and hypernyms (a cook is a person, a pistol a gun and a weapon, a lock a solid object, a meeting an event, "10 m" a measure). IF a class's requirement fails the object (its first sense first, its other senses when no class fits that one), or the class's frames take no object where the line has one, or none takes two where the line has two → THEN the router leaves that sense's tools out. IF a particle follows the verb ("hold on to", "gobble down") → THEN no sense is left out (VerbNet lists phrasal verbs as members of their own). IF two objects follow a verb of giving or throwing (NP V NP NP: "throw the cook a coin") → THEN Transfer. IF a person follows a verb of fulfilling without a with-phrase ("trust the cook") → THEN no hand-over. IF a blow verb has another sense the object explicitly fits that names another tool ("I charge the cook": attack, or bill him: Price), or two objects ("I cut the cook a slice of bread") → THEN code drafts no blow; the planner reads the sense from the scene. Corpus priors stay out: in SemLink's annotated news text "fire" is dismissal 28 times in 36 and "charge" mostly billing, which would push a roleplay to the news senses. Check: 272 player lines (sections 36–38 corpora, the route test), 6 routings changed, each one leaving out a sense the object cannot meet; the inventory of 16,224 phrases (every verb with two or more senses × a noun of each of 22 kinds, and two more shapes) in the bundle (`sense-inventory.tsv`).

**Results as the narrator reads them (2026-10-27 (105); `rules-tools.ts`, `question-pass.ts`, `body-engine.ts`, `drugs.ts`, `chem.ts`).**
IF a result reaches the narrator (its own tool call, or [DECIDED]) → THEN it carries the outcome and its cause in words: no sensitivity note, no status tag, no level in dB ("normal voice at 1 m, quiet room"), no compound's formula, CAS number or molar mass, no program or model name ("(Pulse)", "for the 77 kg model"), no death chance for a burn (the engine draws the death: `advance`), no blood-loss class while no blood is lost. IF Weather has no calendar date → THEN it asks for "time" as YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM, the question pass's form; IF the place has no climate data → THEN it asks for the nearest listed town. Check: every result the engine test makes (10,770 texts from 42 tools and blocks): 2,189 texts with a narrator-facing hit when the narrator calls the tools itself, 2,150 through [DECIDED] (dB 1,520, blood class at 0 L 583, status tags 84, sensitivity notes 40, constant names 27, sources 28) → 0.

**The tools' words as the scene allows them (2026-10-27 (104); `scene-fit.ts`, `rules-tools.ts`, `question-pass.ts`, `plan-schema.ts`, `social.ts`, `rules-audit.ts`, `index.ts`).**
IF an option, an argument or a tool exists only from a year (or up to one), or only in a world with magic → THEN the planner's catalogue, its schema, the narrator's tool schemas and the narrator's brief hold it only in such a scene; an unknown year keeps everything. Years: a treatment from when it first worked as modelled (transfusion 1901, antivenom 1895, antibiotics 1941, laparotomy 1809, oxygen 1917, hyperbaric oxygen 1956), a drug from its first medical use (adrenaline 1901 … rocuronium 1994), anaesthesia 1846, the survey of acts from 2000 (the tool's own rule), a faith from its founding, SARS 2002, a coin while it circulates and an epidemic from its start (their labels), Price & Bouffard's situations from when each exists, aluminium 1886, mild steel 1856, the measured ensembles from 1960, a handgun shot and its arguments from 1350 [ASSUMED where marked in `scene-fit.ts`]; Battle 1600–1991, NameCharacter from 1910 and in the United States only, Price 1209–2008, a market only in the years its data cover. Adamantine and CastSpell only with magic.
IF the question pass did not run → THEN the narrator is offered the tools the router finds in the player's line, with those every turn may need (all the scene's tools when it finds none), each with the scene's arguments (SillyTavern reads a tool's parameters after `shouldRegister`).
IF a condemned act is named → THEN code reads the survey act from the words ("he killed himself": suicide); the list of 19 acts goes back only when the words match none or several.
Examples name what any scene holds, or a placeholder the scene fills; species is "a creature name" (was "DF creature"); the brief's speech line reads "Humans talk, and so do the peoples [WORLD] adds and the bodies it marks as talking". Check: six ordinary lines in 1300, 1850 and 1990: planner 4,088 → 3,568–3,669 tokens a line, narrator (no question pass) 23,180 → 2,723–2,882, out-of-scene words 21 and 42 → 0 in 1300 and 1850; `tools/prompt-audit.mjs` 15 → 9 sentences.

**A shot by every wording, and fire by its object (2026-10-27 (103); `verbs.ts`, `router.ts`).**
IF a verb or a light verb's noun routes by a FrameNet frame of shooting (Hit_target, Use_firearm, Shoot_projectiles) → THEN the turn is offered both tools the shot route names: BodyStrike (a launcher) and BodyCheck (shot_m, a handgun) (`toolsNamed`; before, the first word only, so "I fire my pistol at the guard", "take a shot at", "take aim" never reached shot_m). IF fire's object (the words before a preposition) is a person or a thing baked or lit and names no weapon or missile → THEN no shot (PropBank's frameset fire: fire.01's object is the gun or projectile, fire.02's the one let go, FrameNet Firing; fire.03 bakes pots, fire.04 ignites; things fired [ASSUMED list]: kiln, oven, stove, furnace, forge, boiler, engine, thrusters, clay, pots, pottery, bricks, glaze). "Fire up" is a start, no shot (a named verb's empty list is its answer). "Open fire", "return fire" and "gun … down" are shots; "hold (my) fire" is no grip. IF a frame noun stands before of and a thing as a measure ("a shot of whiskey": grammar.ts measureOfGoods) → THEN no act. Check: route 172 (11 new lines, each missing on section 37's router); engine N65 task routes "fire the cook", "take a shot of whiskey" → none, "fire the musket at the deer" → BodyStrike.

### 7.4 Scoring the question pass

`/rules-eval [n]` sends the first n of 50 scenes (`eval-scenes.ts`; default all) to the State Model as question-pass requests, with a fixed tracker state (two characters, a date in 1850; five scenes carry their own date), and scores the calls: a call counts only when it would run (known tool, required arguments, schema check passed). Each scene lists the calls a correct reading needs and the arguments that decide their outcomes (equal, contains, range, list holds a name, list length); other arguments are free, recording a fact is always allowed, and a scene may allow further tools. Scenes 40–44 need no call. Since 2026-10-27 (109) the eval offers tools as play does: the router's and the scene's words' when the router routes a verb, the picker's otherwise (before, it always asked the picker, a step play skips whenever the router routes).

| Measure | Definition |
|---|---|
| fully correct | every expected call made with every deciding argument right, and no call outside the allowed ones |
| tool precision | matched calls ÷ calls made (allowed extras excluded) |
| tool recall | matched calls ÷ expected calls |
| deciding arguments right | passed checks ÷ checks |
| quiet when nothing is in doubt | share of the no-call scenes with no call |

The scenes cover every decision tool once or more (BodyStrike 4, BodyCheck 3, PassTime 4, BodyEnvironment 4, SocialResponse 5). Scorer check: a stand-in model answering with the expected calls scores 50/50; one answering no calls scores 5/50 (the quiet scenes), recall 0 %; inside SillyTavern with the scripted stand-in model the command runs and reports. Per-scene results go to the browser console. Together with /rules-stats (narration that departs from results) it measures the two factors §7.3 does not: picking the tool, and filling its deciding arguments.

What these show:
- Edged attacks: a sword, a human bite and a bear's claws cut skin, fat and muscle and stop at bone; a stab reaches the spine and is held.
- Blunt attacks (punch, kick, mace) smash soft tissue and the bone holds at median force; bone breaks in the tail of the spread (§4.6).
- Armour: an iron breastplate or steel greaves stop a sword slash; mail stops the cut and passes a bruise; leather armour is cut through; a mace through an iron helm bruises and the skull holds.
- Bites: a wolf's bite cuts skin, fat and muscle and stops at the shin bone; a human bite cuts to the bone (§4.7).

## 11. Code

**Coverage map (Conceptual Dependency, Schank 1975).** Every action reduces to one of 11 primitive acts; each act has a tool, so an action with no tool is a gap, not a narrator's choice.

| Act | Meaning | Tool |
|---|---|---|
| PTRANS | move oneself or something somewhere | PassTime (walking), Chase |
| PROPEL | apply force | BodyStrike, BodyFall |
| MOVE | move a body part | BodyCheck |
| GRASP | hold | HoldOn |
| INGEST | take in | BodyIntake |
| EXPEL | push out of the body | Sickness (sea crossing, after surgery); Disease (food poisoning) |
| ATRANS | transfer possession | Transfer (record); SocialResponse, Choose, Look, HoldOn, BodyStrike, Price (whether and at what cost) |
| MTRANS | pass information | Communicate, RecordFact |
| MBUILD | work something out | WorkOut |
| ATTEND | perceive | Look, Listen |
| SPEAK | make sounds | Communicate |

**Names.** IF a tool names a character in other case or spacing than the record ("mara ") → THEN it is the recorded character. IF a name of 4 or more letters differs by one letter from a recorded one ("Mary" with Mara on record) and the call gives no species → THEN the call returns an error naming the recorded character, so a misspelling cannot split one body into two. A call that gives a species starts a separate character. Labels that differ only in a number (stranger 1, stranger 2) are separate characters.

**Working something out (WorkOut).** Share of university students solving within t seconds: a link between clues 0.565 (1 − e^(−t/13.4 s)), fitted exactly to 7.8 % at 2 s, 23.0 % at 7 s, 50.5 % at 30 s (Bowden & Jung-Beeman 2003, means over 144 problems, 289 participants); a riddle needing a new view 48.3 % at 4 min and a step-by-step problem 58.0 % at 4 min (Webb, Little & Cropper 2016, Table 1, mean of three experiments), on the same curve shape stretched ×8 so 4 min sits where 30 s does [ASSUMED]. The character's standing among students (a share band) shifts the log-odds by the band's odds [ASSUMED]; the body state applies through alertness. With no `share` from the story, the character's own general ability gZ, normal and drawn once, places them among the students of the norms, who stood above everyone by the year of the study: US undergraduates' Flynn-adjusted IQ = 490.742 − 0.192 × year (Uttl, Violo & Gibson 2024, 106 samples, 9,902 students), so 106.2 (z 0.41) for the 2003 word-link norms and 103.7 (z 0.24) for the 2016 problem norms; Φ(gZ − z) is the share of students they beat (before: z = 1 for all).

**Sickness.** Sea crossing of 0.5–7 h: vomits 5.0 % male, 8.8 % female, 12.0 % under 15, 7.0 % unknown; of the rest, 24.4 % feel seasick without vomiting ((21.3 + 4.3 + 4.1 − 7.0) / 93.0, every vomiter counted as unwell [ASSUMED]) (Lawther & Griffin 1988, 20,029 passengers). After general anaesthesia, nausea or vomiting within 24 h with 0–4 risk factors (female, earlier motion sickness or PONV, non-smoker, opioids after): 10, 21, 39, 61, 79 % (Apfel et al. 1999).

**Evidence in lie judgement (OPEN-89 narrowed).** IF the listener holds evidence bearing on the statement → THEN kept until the speaker has told their story: 85.0 % of truths and 85.7 % of lies judged correctly; revealed early: 57.1 % and 55.0 % (Hartwig, Granhag, Strömwall & Kronkvist 2006, 82 interviewers; the late group was trained in the strategic use of evidence). Without evidence the base rates stay (Bond & DePaulo 2006).

**A feeling that lasts (2026-10-22).** Appraise on a tracked character keeps the main emotion in [BODY] ("still feels distress (the blow)") for a drawn time, exponential with the median hours below [ASSUMED shape]; PassTime runs it down. The median is scaled by event importance and sex from the same study (across emotions mean log10 duration rose 0.14 per point of a 0–6 importance scale, B = .14; women 4.42 against men 4.04, medians 5.04 h and 2 h; 112 women, 118 men): × 10^(0.14 (I − 4.5)) with I = 3 for an event that touches a goal and 6 for a central one [ASSUMED mapping], × 10^(4.42 − 4.225) for a woman and 10^(4.04 − 4.225) for a man. Check, distress (pooled median 48 h): a woman's about a central event 127 h, a man's about an event that touches a goal 20 h (300 draws each; expected 122 and 19). Check: distress (median 48 h) over 600 draws: the draws' quantiles are uniform (median 0.487).

**How long an emotion lasts (OPEN-92 narrowed).** Median hours to the first return to baseline (Verduyn & Lavrijsen 2015, 233 high-school students, 27 emotions, Fig. 1): each OCC type takes the nearest measured emotion [mapping ASSUMED] — distress 48 (sadness), hope 27, anger, pride and gratification 24, joy, happy-for and gratitude 6, fear, self-reproach and remorse 4 (anxiety, guilt), satisfaction 3 (contentment), fears-confirmed 2.3 (desperation), resentment and disappointment 2, sorry-for, appreciation and reproach 1.5, relief and disliking 0.8. The narrator gets it in words (fades within the hour; some hours; about a day; days), longer the more the event matters (event importance, the paper's strongest predictor). Intensity scales stay open.

**Fingertip holds (OPEN-90 narrowed).** IF only the fingertips hold (a ledge, a sill) → THEN grip × four-fingertip force over grip: non-climbers 309/440, recreational climbers 359/445, elite 447/526 N (Grant et al. 1996, 30 men).

**Cold and thinking (OPEN-83 narrowed).** WorkOut is a timed complex task, so a cooled core slows it; simple attention and recognition held after 2–4 °C of central cooling while working-memory and processing tasks fell, and cold water itself changed nothing (Giesbrecht et al. 1993).

**Papers read, not used yet.** Parlange & Katz 2000 (wind and dew point in the WGEN generator; parameters are fitted per station, so a world-wide use needs station data: OPEN-91 stays). Bohannon 1997 (comfortable and maximum walking speed by age and sex; used since 2026-10-25 for hurrying, below), Denny 2008 and Srinivasan 2009 (speed limits and optimal gaits; no sprint norms for untrained adults). Hånell & Rostami 2020 (a knockout ends "within a few minutes or less", no distribution): OPEN-81 stays.

**The player's line read by its grammar (2026-10-25 (93); `grammar.ts`, `data/wordnet.ts`).** A word is read in the construction it stands in, by the rule Huddleston & Pullum's *Cambridge Grammar of the English Language* (2002, CGEL) states for that construction; where the rule asks a fact about one word, Open English WordNet 2024 gives it. (1) Light verbs (CGEL ch. 4 §7, pp. 290–296): give, make, have, take, do, and offer, pay, put, raise with fewer nouns, are light when "the main semantic content is located … in the noun functioning as head of the direct object", a noun related to an associated verb: "She gave him a kiss" says "She kissed him". The reader takes a use as light when CGEL's own samples hold the noun for that verb ([10]–[21], p. 296), when WordNet lists the phrase as a verb (give chase, give way, take a breath, have a look: 170 idioms), or when the noun's most frequent WordNet sense is an act derived from a verb and it has no sense naming a thing; as ordinary (a hand-over, a taking) when the noun names only things, when the object is definite ("the most usual determiner with light verbs is the indefinite article", p. 291), or when an of phrase names a thing ("a slice of pie", "a cup of tea": quantity and partitive nouns, ch. 5 §3.3 and p. 411) — except after the physical acts of give [11], which "do not normally take PP complements" (p. 294), so "a pat of butter" is butter; a noun that names both an act and a thing ("a ring", "a hand"; "I had a bath": "in some cases there is ambiguity between the two uses", p. 291) is left to the scene. A light use is read as its associated verb, the indirect object as that verb's object ([1], [9]–[13]): "I give Tom a quick kick in the shin" is drafted as a blow at Tom, "I take a walk to the market, 300 yards" as a walk, "I take a sip of the wine" routes drinking, and none of them a hand-over. (2) Understood subjects (CGEL ch. 14 §§1.7, 3, 5): in the complex catenative construction the NP between the verbs does the act of the non-finite ("Pat persuaded Kim to travel by bus", "Pat intended Kim to travel by bus"), with to (Classes 2A, 2B, 3A, 3B: tell, persuade, want, expect, order, allow…) or a bare infinitival (make, let, have, help and the verbs of perception: "I watch Tom climb the wall", "I make Tom walk"), and the oblique of prepositional verbs ("I rely on Tom to keep watch", 3Aiii, 2Aiv); promise keeps its subject (p. 1229). After a verb with two objects the infinitival is a purpose adjunct whose doer the meaning settles (§4.4; [17] of §5.3), so code writes it for no one. (3) Negation (CGEL ch. 9): the verbal negators; the "covertly negative" verbs whose complement is understood as not happening (§4.4, pp. 835–836: avoid, decline, fail, forget, neglect, omit, refrain; keep, prevent, stop, prohibit, ban, hinder … from); without and too … to (p. 837); almost, nearly, practically ("a strong negative implicature", p. 723); refuse (Karttunen 2012, a one-way implicative). Negated, the two-way implicatives fail, forget, neglect, avoid and refrain say the act was done ("I didn't forget to lock the door"; Karttunen 2012, Table 1). (4) Pronouns (CGEL ch. 17 §3.1, p. 1484; agreement, ch. 5): "him" and "her" are the one person named before them whose sex fits, not the subject of their own clause ("the absence of a reflexive in Ann blames her … excludes Ann"), nor the player in a line in the first person; with two who fit the grammar does not choose, and the scene does ("Tom punches me. I hit him back" is a blow at Tom; "Tom and Will shove me. I hit him back" is drafted for no one). (5) Auxiliaries (CGEL ch. 3 §2): a modal, or be, get or have before a participle, is no lexical verb; the verb after it is read. Modality (ch. 3 §9.1, p. 173: a modalised clause does not commit the speaker to the act): under can, could, may, might, must, should, would, ought, would rather and had better an act is not done, save can or could with a verb of perception ("I can hear something rattling", [24ii] p. 185); will and shall are the act about to be done [ASSUMED for roleplay]. A modal before its subject asks ("Can I climb the wall?"). (6) Conditions (ch. 8 §14, pp. 738–760; the other conditional words, [61] p. 758): an act in a sentence with if, unless, provided, as long as, on condition, assuming, supposing, in the event or in case is not done. (7) The passive (ch. 16 §10, pp. 1427–1430): be or get with a past participle; the subject undergoes the act and the doer is the complement of by — the player's only when that is "me" or their name; a participle that is its own base ("hit") counts with a by phrase after it. (8) Entailment of the complex catenatives (Karttunen 2012, Table 2; [15iii] p. 1229; [43] p. 1236): make, have, get, force, compel, cause, help, let and the verbs of perception say the NP's act happens; want, tell, ask, expect, allow and the rest do not. (9) Quantity nouns (ch. 5 §3.3): "a N1 of N2" with N2 bare is an amount of N2; with N1 a quantity or a thing and N2 a thing (WordNet), a measure of goods with a price. (10) A capitalised word inside a sentence is a name, no verb (ch. 5 §20, proper names); one opening a sentence is a subject when it is a given name or no common word ("Tom punches me"). (11) Where the subject stands (ch. 4 §3.1, p. 243: "The basic position is before the predicator"; ch. 8 §20.1, pp. 779–780: "front position is before the subject"): the subject is the nominative pronoun (nominative only as "subject of a finite clause", ch. 5 §16.2.1, p. 458) or name just before the verb or its auxiliary, after an adjunct in front position — a PP (the prepositions of ch. 7 [27], p. 613, and the central ones it leaves out [ASSUMED]), a deictic time word or frequency adverb (ch. 8 [6] p. 695, [9i] p. 715), or an NP whose head is a noun of WordNet's noun.time ("The next day", "Every morning", "Two days ago"); a reduced relative ("The man I hit runs") is no front adjunct. (12) Time (ch. 3 §4.2.1, pp. 127–128): the simple present of a dynamic verb is a single act now only in performatives and commentaries, and a player's line is a commentary on their own acts ("A demonstration … can be thought of as a commentary on one's own actions"), so its present is the act now; a non-bounding frequency adjunct makes a serial state, a habit (ch. 8 §9, pp. 713–715: always, frequently, invariably, normally, often, rarely, regularly, seldom, sometimes, usually; each or every with a noun of time; whenever; N times a day; as a rule, for the most part — constantly, repeatedly, occasionally and the like are left out because they can count the blows of one occasion [ASSUMED split]); a deictic adjunct of a past or future time (ch. 8 §6.2 [6], p. 695; ch. 3 §4.3.1 p. 137, §4.2.4 pp. 131–133) puts the act then; used to (ch. 3 §2.5.9, p. 115), be about to, be on the point of (§10.2 n. 68, p. 211) and will or shall with the perfect (§10.1 [4i], p. 210: "He will have left already" is in past time, will the modal judgement) likewise; a clause takes its own adjunct, else the one of the clause before it in its sentence, and "now", "today", "tonight" say now; the preterite without such an adjunct is the turn's narrative tense [ASSUMED for roleplay]. (13) Clause type (ch. 10): a closed interrogative (subject–auxiliary inversion, with do, §2 [1]–[2], p. 856; the subject a pronoun or a name, matched with its case, so "I will just walk" is no question), an open one, or a declarative ending in a question mark (§4.7.2, p. 881) asks: its act is not done, save a question of perception, which the look or the listening answers [ASSUMED]; a 1st person inclusive let-imperative is "a proposal for joint action" (§9.3.2, p. 936): said, its act offered and not drafted; a sentence opening with a vocative set off by a comma and a verb in its plain form (§9.2.2, p. 927) is a directive to that person: speech to them, and the act theirs if they comply. (14) The prepositional alternant of the ditransitive (ch. 4 §4.3 [7], p. 248: "I sent Sue a copy" ~ "I sent a copy to Sue"): give, offer, hand and pass with no indirect object take the recipient from a to phrase after the object (a particle may stand between: "hand the letter back to the captain"); the light uses keep their own complements. (15) "It" (ch. 5 §17.2.2, p. 488: it "for entities which are neither male nor female", animals among them) handed over is the one thing or animal the line names before it as an object, agreeing in number ("them" with a number: "two apples"); with two, the scene settles it. A person introduced by a noun phrase is an antecedent (ch. 5 §6.1 [6iii], p. 370: "the first sentence introduces a particular cat into the discourse"): "A man walks in. I punch him." strikes the man, the phrase's head its last person noun ("a drunk sailor"), its sex from the head (census.ts wordSex: man and men are male). The phrase after to, at, with, from, ask, tell, greet or give is one only when its words name a person or an animal (verbs.ts creatureAfter, as a blow's object is read) and it is no pronoun; one met as "a monk" is "the monk", and the phrase's second word is read ahead, so a word after it that takes a person starts the next phrase (2026-10-26 (101); before, "with a lantern" and "at the bar" stood for him: "A sailor walks in with a lantern. I punch him." drafted no blow, "I sit at the bar with my friend. I punch him." struck the bar; frame R47). A reflexive object is the subject of its own verb (ch. 17 §3.1 [3i], p. 1484): "I hit myself" strikes the player. Coordinated noun phrases are one target (ch. 15 §1 [2i], p. 1275: an NP-coordination is "functionally like an NP"; "I see Tom and Will"). (16) Information packaging (ch. 2 §16 [1], p. 67; ch. 16 §9): an it-cleft and a pseudo-cleft are read as their basic counterparts ("It is Tom that I hit" as "I hit Tom", "What I do is walk to the door" as "I walk to the door"); a relative clause with an object of its own after its verb is extraposition, not a cleft ("It is a fact that I hit Tom"). (17) Order (ch. 15 §2.2.3, pp. 1299–1300: "Where the coordinates denote occurrences rather than states, the linear order generally reflects the temporal sequence of the events", across sentences too): code's calls are written in the order the line tells the acts, an act in a before-clause after its sentence's others and one in an after-clause before them. (18) Auxiliaries take central adverbs after them ("I have just walked": ch. 8 §20.1 [3]); "used to" is an auxiliary of past aspect, no using. (19) Where a walk ends (ch. 8 §4.3, pp. 684–688; grammar.ts walkEnd), the walk's step `to`:
- **Goal.** A goal of to, into or onto is the final location ("the final locations are (in) Bonn and in her bedroom, with to being a marker of the goal category"). "To" before a verb is the infinitive's marker, no goal.
- **Other place phrases as goals.** "Most of the expressions that can function as location can also function as goal" ([27]). These keep their preposition ("behind the house", "near the fire"); in and on give the place itself, and at its complement.
- **Across.** Across alone is a goal on the other side (n. 7): "far side of" the place.
- **Direction.** Towards gives only "a potential final location": a spot its distance towards the place.
- **Path.** A path (along, through, past, via, up, down, over, around; by with a walk is past) gives an intermediate place: a spot its distance along it.
- **Source.** A source (away from, from, off; out of: outside the place) gives the start: a spot its distance from it.
- **There.** "There" with no goal is the place a locative phrase named before it (ch. 17 §9.1, pp. 1549–1550: "Kim is in Paris and wants us to go there too").
- **No end said.** A walk with no end in the words ends at a spot its distance from where the walker stood (an omitted goal is "contextually understood", [20]); it ends outside only when the words say out, outside, leave or exit.
- **The goal's noun phrase.** It keeps a post-head PP of place ("the market on Wentworth Street"). It ends at a coordinator, a subordinator, an infinitive's to, another PP of motion, a manner adverb, or a PP of time (in, on, at, within before a noun of WordNet's noun.time: "to the door in a minute").
- **Legs.** Each leg of a walk in legs is read the same way.

(20) A walk the words time and do not end is time spent walking (ch. 3 §3.2.3 [18], p. 122: "He played tennis for an hour" against "*He walked a mile for an hour"; ch. 8 §4.3 [21], p. 684: a goal bounds the walk, a path does not):
- "I walk for an hour" and "I run for ten minutes" draft PassTime with the walker (running for run, ran, jog), which settles the routed Act.
- A goal or a distance makes the walk an accomplishment, so a for-phrase gives it no duration.

(21) Resultatives (ch. 4 §5.3, p. 260): an optional resultative entails the act without it ("He washed it clean entails He washed it"). The reader reads the act ("I knock the guard unconscious": the blow), and the result is the outcome the engine decides, an attempt by the question pass's rule.

(22) The one spoken to (2026-10-26 (99)) is a noun phrase, its head after up to two modifiers ("the old blind beggar", "the drunk sailor"): of the words after the article, up to three and none a preposition or a conjunction, the run that ends at the last one naming a person, an animal or a group of people. Before, only the word after the article was read, so "the old" was no person and nothing was drafted. A group of people is spoken to as a person is: a noun whose most frequent sense WordNet files below social group ("people sharing some social relation") or people (crew, patrol, council, jury, crowd; `tools/build-wordnet.mjs`, kind h). A group of things is not (table, class, herd), and a blow still lands on a person, not on a group ("I hit the board" is no blow).

(23) Markdown emphasis (2026-10-26 (101)) is how a line is set, not its words: in SillyTavern * and _ mark actions and italics. A run of one to three * or _ paired around words goes before the line is read (frame.ts `unEmphasis`, in `inStory` and in `framedCalls`); a _ only at a word's edge, since CommonMark reads no emphasis inside a word ("foo_bar_"; CommonMark Spec 0.31.2, §6.2, rules 1–4), so snake_case stays, and a lone * stays (2*3). Before, "I punch **Tom**.", "I talk to *Rob* about the horse.", "I _whisper_ to Will" and "I wait *ten* minutes." drafted nothing, and "again" after "*I punch Tom.*" was read back with its asterisks (frame R48).

(24) Quoted words (2026-10-26 (102); `quotes.ts`): every reader of the player's words (router, frame, intent, grammar, prosody) takes the same quotation marks as quoting speech: English "…" and “…”, German „…“ with ‚…‘ inside or »…« with ›…‹ inside, Swiss «…» (Bier 2006, typokurz, §2), and «…» with ‹…› as in French, the single ones the Unicode names call quotation marks (U+2039, U+203A); single ‘…’ and '…' are left to apostrophes. Their words are what is said: "Er sagte: ›Climb the wall!‹" routes Communicate and no climb, "I say to Tom, «Run away!»" gives the message "Run away!", and a slip inside them is left as said. Before, only "…" and “…” were quotes: words in guillemets routed as the player's acts and were respelt, and a slip after a closed "…" was taken as inside it (route R57, frame R58, intent R59). A cut-off before a closing mark or bracket is one ("›War-‹", "(wai-)"; ui R60), and the speech whose loudness sets the effort is the words in any quotes (ui R61).

(25) An Act's action (2026-10-26 (102)) starts at its motion verb, as the Act tool takes it (actions.ts MOTION_START), and leaves out the sentences after it that make no move: "Today I walk 10 m to the gate" is "walk 10 m to the gate", "I punch Tom. I walk 10 m to the door" is "walk 10 m to the door", and "*I walk 10 m to the door.* Hello there." is "walk 10 m to the door."; the verb is read outside quoted words. Before, the note under the line read "Move punch Tom. I walk 10 m to the door" (frame R56); the walk itself was the same.

Tests: route, frame (grammar cases); controls G1–G57, G59–G103, R22b, Q7b, N65d, Q11h, RT1, RT4, RW12–RW15, RY14–RY17, RY21, RY22, RZ14–RZ24.

**The line as the player meant it (2026-10-26 (97); `intent.ts`).** A player's line is the written, lossy form of what they have in mind. Before the grammar reads it, the line is read as a listener reads speech, by a noisy channel: the meaning is the one that best explains the words, given what the scene makes likely (Shannon 1948; Levy 2008; Gibson, Bergen & Piantadosi 2013).
- **(1) Slips.** A lower-case form of four letters or more that is no word is read as the word one slip away (Damerau 1964: a deletion, insertion, substitution or swap) with the highest prior. A "word" here is in the lexicon, a VerbNet verb's form, a word of the scene, or a prefix and a word.
  - Forms two slips away are left out: typists leave 1.167 % of characters wrong (Dhakal, Feit, Kristensson & Oulasvirta 2018), so a second slip in a six-letter word is about 3 % as likely as a first.
  - The prior gives `sceneWordShare` 0.5 [ASSUMED] to the scene's words (its people's names and the content words of the narrator's last messages) and spreads the rest over the lexicon's 39,952 words.
  - Before a determiner, the prepositions one slip away are preferred, since the noun phrase that follows needs one (Levy 2008: the grammar is part of the prior; CGEL ch. 7 §2).
  - A reading is taken at `readAsMeantAt` 0.5 of belief [ASSUMED, as `askBelowSafe`], and never when two words tie.
- **(2) A dropped "to".** Give, hand, pass, offer, throw, toss or send, then a thing, then a person with no preposition between, is read with "to" between: the literal reading gives a person to a thing (Gibson et al. 2013; CGEL ch. 4 §4.3).
- **(3) The last act.** A line that is only "again", "once more", "I do it again", "the same", "the same to X" or "that to X" is the player's last line, with X as the one it is done to (CGEL ch. 17 §7.6 [51], p. 1532: "I hope they don't do that to me" is "question me").
- **(4) "Him" and "her"** with no one named before them in the line.
  - Weighed by P(person | pronoun) ∝ P(pronoun | person) · P(person) (Kehler, Kertz, Rohde & Elman 2008; Rohde & Kehler 2014).
  - P(pronoun | person) is `pronounForSubject` 0.69 for the subject of the latest sentence that names a candidate, and `pronounForOther` 0.185 for another person in that sentence (Rosa & Arnold 2017, J Mem Lang 94, Exp. 2, written continuations, same-gender context, 425 references).
  - It is `pronounForUnfocused` 0.01 for a person outside that sentence [ASSUMED from Gundel, Hedberg & Zacharski 1993: of 215 tokens of "it", 214 had a referent in focus, one a referent only activated].
  - P(person) is flat: in story continuations whose verbs favour neither, subject and object are mentioned next about equally often (Bader & Portele 2019, German: 18 % and 20 %) [ASSUMED for English roleplay lines].
  - The weights go to the chooser (ask.ts), which takes or asks by its thresholds.
  - "Back" after the pronoun is "in repayment or retaliation" (Open English WordNet 2024, back, adverb, sense 6), so the person is the subject of the narrator's last sentence whose object is the player.
- **(5) A goal stated in the player's recent lines** ("I'm looking for X", "I want to find X", "I search for X") stays in force until another is stated (Zwaan & Radvansky 1998, the situation model's intentionality dimension; Baker, Saxe & Tenenbaum 2009, goals inferred by inverse planning). It fills a search with no "for", and it is told to the planner.
- **(6) The lexicon.** It now holds the prefixed words its Hunspell sources build by prefix flags and its build dropped: 3,076 that WordNet lists, among them "return", "unable" and "injury". It also holds the grammar's prepositions ("into" was missing).

Each reconstruction is shown under the line and told to the planner. Tests: tests/intent.test.mjs, engine I1, ui; controls I1–I19, L1–L3.

**The reader against the grammar's chapters (2026-10-25 (95)).** Each chapter of CGEL a player's line draws on, with what the reader reads by that chapter's rule and what it leaves to the scene or the planner. Probe lines: a set per chapter, read through the router and the frame reader.

| CGEL chapter | Read by its rule | Not read (the scene or the planner settles it) |
|---|---|---|
| 2 §16, 16 Information packaging | The passive (§10); it-clefts and pseudo-clefts as their basic counterparts (§9) | Preposing ("Tom I punch"), dislocation, existential there |
| 3 The verb | Auxiliaries and modals (§§2, 9.1); the present as the act now, habits, past and future times, used to, about to (§§2.5.9, 4.2, 4.3.1, 10.2) | The perfect and the progressive beyond that ("I was walking when Tom hit me" is the walk) |
| 4 Complements | Light verbs (§7); the ditransitive and its prepositional alternant (§4.3); resultatives, whose act is read and whose result the engine decides (§5.3) | — |
| 5 Nouns and noun phrases | Quantities and partitives (§3.3, p. 411); a person a noun phrase introduces (§6.1); gender of he, she, it (§17); names (§20) | "They" and "them" for people; quantified objects ("I punch every guard", "both guards") |
| 7 Prepositions | The prepositions opening a front adjunct ([27]) | — |
| 8 Adjuncts and complements of place | Front position (§20.1); conditions (§14); frequency and deictic time (§§6.2, 9); goal, source, path and direction of a walk (§4.3); duration of an unbounded walk (§4.3 [21], with ch. 3 [18]) | Manner ("I walk quietly": the pace verbs read it, the adverbs not), a bounding frequency's count ("I hit Tom twice") |
| 9 Negation | Verbal and covert negation, the proximatives (§§2, 4.4) | — |
| 10 Clause type | Questions (§§2, 4.7.2), let-imperatives (§9.3), vocative directives (§9.2.2) | Exclamatives (no act to read) |
| 13 Comparison | — | Comparatives ("I walk faster than Tom") change no argument code writes |
| 14 Non-finite clauses | Understood subjects of the catenatives (§§1.7, 3, 5) and which entail their act | — |
| 15 Coordination | The order of coordinated occurrences (§2.2.3); coordinated targets (§1) | — |
| 17 Deixis and anaphora | Him and her (§3.1), reflexives (§3.1), it for a thing handed over, there as the place named before (§9.1) | "Here", deictic "there" with no place named, and "this" and "that" point at what only the scene shows |

**Pace in the player's words (2026-10-25).** The verb sets an Act's pace when code writes the Act: run, jog → a run; sprint, dash → a sprint [ASSUMED for dash]; hurry, rush, hasten → the fastest walk; creep, sneak, tiptoe → a creep. A run goes at `runFactor` 0.46 of the runner's own top speed: free-living runners chose the speed that costs them least energy per metre, 2.65 m/s for women and 3.35 m/s for men among healthy young adults (Selinger, Hicks, Jackson, Wall-Scheffler, Chang & Delp 2022, "Running in the wild: Energetics explain ecological running speeds", Current Biology 32:2309–2315, as reported by Popular Science; the paper's own pages, its PubMed Central copy and the authors' data page on SimTK give no figures that could be read here), over the engine's mean top speed at 25 (5.79 and 7.27 m/s): 0.457 and 0.461 [fitted, range 0.40–0.55]. The fastest walk is the walker's walking speed × Bohannon 1997's maximum over comfortable speed for their sex and decade (Age Ageing 26:15, 230 healthy adults over 7.62 m, Table 4, read in full on 2026-10-25 from the academia.edu copy: all 24 means as here: men 20s 253.3/139.3 … 70s 207.9/133.0 cm/s; women 20s 246.7/140.7 … 70s 174.9/127.2; under 20 the 20s' share, from 80 the 70s'). Check: a 25-year-old woman walks at 1.34 m/s and hurries at 2.35; a 75-year-old man 1.26 and 1.97. A walk given in legs, each with its distance and end ("5 m to the door, then 10 m to the gate"), is walked leg by leg; a distance that is no leg ("2 km from the river") stays out. A verb the line says is not done sets no pace and makes no move (`undone()`: not, never, a negated auxiliary, refuse to, almost, nearly, without [ASSUMED list]): "I don't run. I walk 10 m to the door." walks, "I don't walk 10 m to the door" moves no one (2026-10-25 (92)). Tests R14, R19, Q13a–Q13c.

**Human running speed (OPEN-90 narrowed).** Each human keeps one draw (runZ) from untrained adults of their sex: flying 20 m in 3.6 ± 0.4 s for 149 women and 2.9 ± 0.3 s for 84 men, mean age 43 (Manderoos 2020, University of Helsinki doctoral thesis, Study II, Table 4): 20.0 ± 2.2 and 24.8 ± 2.6 km/h, SD by the delta method, draws held within ±3 SD; sex unknown takes the mean of both. Other ages take the same values [ASSUMED]; training, load, terrain and acceleration stay open. Body fat: fat percentage and 20 m sprint time correlate r = 0.50 (23 male college soccer players, Ishida, Travis & Stone 2021, J Funct Morphol Kinesiol 6:7; 12 elite female sprinters over 5–10 years, partial r = 0.46–0.50, abstract supplied without authors); BMI stands for body fat [ASSUMED], so speed z = −0.5 × BMI z + √0.75 × the person's own draw whenever BMI z is known: drawn by BodySize, or from a build the story states (thin 17.5, average 22, overweight 27.5, obese 32.5 BMI, the middle of each WHO band) against the setting country's mean for that sex and year, else a mean of 24 [ASSUMED]. Simulated: an obese woman outruns a woman of average build in 21 % of races, against 49 % for two women of unknown build. Ding & Jiang 2020 (3,066 Chinese students: BMI against standing long jump r = −0.14 to −0.21 for men, 0.02 to −0.13 for women) and Sedeaud et al. 2014 (BMI of elite runners by event) were read; they measure jumping and elite athletes, not sprint speed in ordinary adults. Other creatures keep the DF gait speed. Simulated: a man catches a woman with a 10 m start in 93 % of chases.

**Weather every turn.** IF the story has a calendar date and the scene a position → THEN the question pass adds a Weather call for that position when the question model did not ask for one, so the day's weather is decided by code before the narrator can describe it (a reply set in Rio de Janeiro, April 1822, described damp clouds with no Weather result). Check (harness, stub model): Rio 14/4/1822 08:00 → Weather call added: high 28 °C, low 19 °C, 0.1 mm rain.

**World book.** The world is real history with stated additions (principle of minimal departure, Ryan 1980; minimal-rewrite rule, Tetlock & Belkin 1996). It belongs to the character card (all its chats share it; a group chat keeps its own). Each entry: what, kind (creature, people, place, institution, technology, magic, event), since (a year, or always), area (worldwide, or the latitude and longitude of its centre with a radius in km, default 300), cause (one sentence), reaches (areas of life from a closed list of 9), reflections (1–15 facts everyone knows, each in a reached area), and for a creature or people: the DF creature its body is, speaks, active (day, night, any time), weakness (none, sunlight turns it to stone, fire). IF an area is not reached → THEN it is as in real history. IF an entry sets speaks for a DF creature → THEN Communicate follows it over the raws. IF an entry is placed → THEN it is placed by coordinates, never a name: a name means different things at different dates (Norway under Danish rule, modern Norway) and a salient name pulls the narrator toward its associations. The narrator gets the area relative to the scene: here (the scene is inside it), near (its edge within 300 km, about ten days on foot at 30 km a day [ASSUMED]) with a compass direction, or far away with a direction; coordinates only while the scene's position is unknown. The line drafted from is read in its words: the voice marks hidden in it (voice-code.ts) say how a line is spoken, which an entry does not hold, so neither the model nor an entry saved from no answer gets them (2026-10-26 (101); ui R50). The draft prompt asks for cause and reflections without present-day country names, naming a place only as people there at that date would. The scene's position comes from the last place a tool was given (a WMO city or latitude and longitude), or from the question pass ("at": [lat, lon]) when the scene or scenario says where it is. [WORLD] goes to the question model and the narrator each turn; Claim's in_world is judged against real history plus [WORLD]. Entries start from one typed line (`/world-add`, or the panel's Fill): the model drafts the fields, the user edits and approves; the schema check (closed lists, a DF species, 1–15 reflections) runs before saving. Active and weakness are recorded and shown to the narrator; no code acts on them yet.

**A person gone from history (`/world-without Name, year`).** Inventions: Wikipedia, "Timeline of historic inventions" (saved 2026-10-12): 390 dated entries, 213 with a named inventor read by pattern from the entry text (the subject before "invents/develops/builds…", or "by …" but not "works by …"); extraction errors stay possible and the user approves every entry. IF the person is removed from a year → THEN each of their inventions from that year on: IF a co-inventor is listed, or Wikipedia's "List of multiple discoveries" names the person with a word of the invention → THEN made anyway by the other inventor (at the other dated year when one is given). ELSE nobody else makes it with probability e^−1.3 = 27 %: the number of independent inventors is Poisson, mean 1.4 for Merton and Barber's 264 multiples and 1.2 for Ogburn and Thomas's 148 (Simonton 1979, J Pers Soc Psychol 37:1603, as reported by Garfield 1980, Current Contents 44:5, "somewhat more than unity and definitely less than two"); 1.3 is the mean of the two fits, and by Poisson splitting the others, given one inventor, are Poisson(1.3). ELSE made later by someone else, the delay drawn from the gaps between the first two dated discoverers of the listed multiples (110 paragraphs, 17th century on; median 1 year, 75 % within 4, 90 % within 25; same-year multiples count 0). Drawn once per chat (seeded), written as a world-book event the user edits and approves; the first 7 outcomes become its reflections.
